#6757. The 10 Best Hair Wavers Bazaar Editors Are Actually Using

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Get beach hair all year round with these top-rated tools

As someone with pin-straight Asian hair, I have always admired waves and curls. I have slick, straight, stubborn hair, and—most frustratingly—it resists holding a curl. I’ve tried every trick in the book: extra-hold mousses, setting my curls with pins, and the best strong-hold hairsprays. Nothing seemed to do the trick—until I discovered hair wavers, specialized curling irons that create bends instead of twists.

In recent years, hair wavers have grown in popularity because they're easy to use and deliver effortless-looking results. “The effect [of a waver] is less curled and more textured. It’s great for that effortless, tousled vibe or adding body to finger hair without that much bounce,” says celebrity hairstylist Danielle Priano. Ahead, Priano shares her picks for the best hair wavers, along with some of the Bazaar beauty team’s favorites.

Every time my hairstylist uses this waver at the salon, I notice that my waves can stay for up to three to four days, which is considered a long time for my hair type. The waves also look so natural that they give me that “just permed my hair” effect. Priano is a fan of the tool too, saying, “It heats evenly and creates long-lasting waves with a sleek finish.” Plus, it has tons of features like a two-hour auto shutoff and a clear digital display.

You may have seen this on social media, and for good reason: At under $50, it is a great option for budget-conscious users. It’s also highly rated for both long and short hair and is easy (and safe!) for beginners to use because it has no exposed barrel. We also love that it comes in 1- and 1.25-inch wave sizes, so you can choose the wand that best fits your length and style preference.

Priano also swears by Trademark Beauty’s double-barrel wave iron. “This one’s loved for its wider barrels and even heat distribution,” she says. “It’s great for longer hair or when you want that soft glam wave.” As a bonus, the tool also works well for shorter hair as it creates two waves rather than the usual three.

This is my go-to hair waver at the moment because it’s so versatile. You can adjust the barrel size to get the exact wave you want, from tight to soft and beachy. (And you also have full control over the temperature.) I’m super lazy, so I like to opt for something that has full capabilities in one.

You can’t beat this price, which makes Bed Head’s tool a great entry-level styler. That, along with its tourmaline ceramic barrels, 10 heat settings, and easy digital controls, makes it super straightforward to use. The barrels themselves are also on the large side, which creates a great beachy effect in the final look. And if you’re accident-prone, take comfort in the fact that it comes with a protective glove, too.

“This waver is pricey, at nearly $200, but it’s also the most versatile. It comes with three barrels—a waver, a rod, and a clamp—so you can create any type of wave or curl your heart desires,” says Bazaar beauty director Jenna Rosenstein. “I have thick hair, and it works just as well on my hair as it does on those with fine, thin hair, since the heat is customizable up to 450 degrees. It’s also great to travel with!”

This one is another Bazaar editor favorite: “While I do own two different hair wavers, they both serve a unique vibe. I love this INH Triple Barrel when I’m going for a looser, more lived-in wave,” says Sarah Olivieri, Bazaar senior designer. “It really gives the illusion of salty beach hair freshly dried from the sun.”

“I love the look of effortless waves, but creating seamless waves without creases or harsh demarcations between crimps can be tricky,” says Bazaar beauty market and commerce editor Katie Intner. “I love the jumbo Mane waver because the large barrel size gives a more natural wave and crimps hair in under a few minutes. Plus, it’s dual voltage, which is great for travel, and gives a surprising amount of wave longevity.” Even better, the base is detachable and can be used with any of the brand’s other hair tool attachments.

This waver is on the pricey side, but that’s because it has the brand’s next-gen features. Think: even heating and a curved—not cylindrical—shape on the barrels that gives that ideal “effortless” look. They’re also finished with an anodized coating that adds shine and reduces frizz. Who doesn’t want that?

If you aren’t a bona fide hairstyling expert and want a hair waver that doesn’t require much technique, this option from WavyTalk might be a good fit. It has a built-in timer you can set from four to 10 seconds that beeps when it’s time to move on to the next section of hair. Its deep V-shaped plates and dual-barrel design sculpt hair for a more pronounced wave.

Luckily, it’s “fairly straightforward,” Priano says. “You start by sectioning your hair; the smaller the section, the more defined the wave. Then, working from the roots down, clamp the waver onto the hair and hold for a few seconds, usually 5 to 10, depending on your hair type and heat setting. Move down the same strand, overlapping slightly to continue the wave pattern all the way to the ends. Repeat throughout the entire head, and once cooled, you can brush it out for a softer, more lived-in texture or leave it defined for more drama.”

What’s the Best Way to Prep Your Hair Before Using a Waver?

To minimize potential damage, you’ll want to do a few things first: “Always start with clean, dry hair; wavers are not meant for wet styling. I recommend applying a heat protectant from mid-lengths to ends to shield the hair from damage,” Priano adds. “If you’re looking to boost volume and hold, add a lightweight texturizing spray or a mousse before blow-drying. And don’t forget to detangle. Smooth, tangle-free strands help the waver glide better and create more uniform results.”

Is There a Difference Between a Waver and a Curling Iron?

“The main difference between a hair waver and a curling iron lies in the shape and result. A curling iron wraps hair around a single barrel, creating more classic, rounded curls or ringlets, depending on the size of the barrel and the technique,” Priano says. “A waver, on the other hand, presses the hair between barrels to form deep, wave-like ridges.”

#6753. The Streamer’s Guide to a ‘Gilmore Girls’ Thanksgiving

The holiday-themed television episode is a lost art form in the streaming era. Back in the days of appointment television, these episodes became almost ritualistic, holding a distinct place in the annual rhythm of our pop culture consumption. Before we shared streaming passwords, we watched weekly (and traveled by horse and buggy, in the snow, uphill both ways). You actually cared about what your favorite characters dressed up as for Halloween. A few weeks later, your favorite show had a holiday episode— sometimes overt, like every Christmas-timed episode of Glee, and sometimes they were less so.

The holidays offered a time for our favorite shows to reflect on family dynamics and seasonal rituals. For Gilmore Girls, a show entirely based on family dynamics and seasonal rituals, the Thanksgiving holiday was a vessel through which to serve more Stars Hollow kitsch and crispier leaves.

That said…there are a mere two episodes of Gilmore Girls dedicated to Thanksgiving. In truth, the entire series feels like apple pie and stuffing, so any episode will do for Friendsgiving background noise. If you want to do the holiday right, these are the two best episodes to watch on turkey day.

Watch every episode of ‘Gilmore Girls’ on Hulu

In perhaps the most iconic Thanksgiving episode of Gilmore Girls, Rory and Lorelai have a Four Christmases holiday, attending four different celebrations in one day. At the elder Gilmores, it’s lavish, traditional fare. At Mrs. Kim’s house, it’s a strict, no-nonsense, Korean Thanksgiving. At Sookie and Jackson’s, it’s a farm-to-table feast, and at Luke’s diner, it’s a heartfelt bite.

Like any good Thanksgiving dinner, the main course is not the poultry, but the discourse happening at the table. In this episode, Rory reveals to Lorelai that she’s applied to multiple colleges beyond Harvard, opening the door for her to follow her grandfather’s footsteps, straight onto the campus of Yale. Other episode highlights include Lane’s first kiss and growing pains between Human Kirk and Cat Kirk. If you know you know, and if you don’t, thanks for reading this far.

It took three whole seasons to recover from the four-pronged holiday episode, but the girls came back to the table in season 6 with a much more weighted episode. The sixth season of Gilmore Girls is largely marked by the divide between Rory and Lorelai, but this holiday-timed episode marks their reunion after a months long feud. The drama this time is between Luke and Lorelai, with Luke hiding a major secret from his dream girl—he has a daughter named April, whom he’s just met.

In the spirit of journalism, we can’t definitively call any other episode of Gilmore Girls an official Thanksgiving episode. That said, there are more than a few autumnal picks that scratch the same itch.

Like this one, with its supreme autumnal vibes. “They Shoot Gilmores, Don’t They” aired in November, a few weeks before the big season 2 Thanksgiving episode. Here’s an idea: if you start streaming here and make it a mini-marathon, you’ll be feeling festive by Turkey Day.

For millions of Americans, Thanksgiving and football are synonymous whether you’re watching on television or playing in the backyard. So let’s go ahead and call “The One Where Emily and Richard Are Football Fans,” a.k.a. “Ted Koppel’s Big Night Out,” an honorary Thanksgiving episode. The Harvard-Yale game typically happens the Saturday before Thanksgiving. Plus, crisp air, big sweatshirts, and juicy family secrets exposed? Come on!

For cozy nostalgia of a different kind, switch from Hulu/Disney+ to Netflix and queue up the fourth and final episode of the revival series. Honestly, rewatching “A Year in the Life” might also be a good mini-binge idea. Milo Ventimiglia’s yearning face against the autumnal foliage of Stars Hollow is emotional terrorism for the members of Team Jess, but otherwise...

#6748. Women’s Sports Teams Deserve Better Than What Their Homes Are Giving Them

Apparently, we’re going through this...again!

Gotham FC, the current reigning National Women’s Soccer Team champions, are on their way to winning another title. But their journey—which includes the chance to host their first semifinal game at home—has come with a major surprise: a change of venues. After their quarterfinal match against the Seattle Reign FC ended with a messed-up pitch and a fine against Gotham FC due to its conditions, the team was forced to find another place for their next match. The bigger issue? Their only option is a 5,000-person stadium located in Randall’s Island, New York.

Over 11,000 people made their way to Sports Illustrated Stadium—the home to Gotham FC as well as the MLS’s New York Red Bulls—to see them take down Seattle Reign FC. This means that less than half of that audience will be able to catch them at the match that can lead them to the finals.

While pitch conditions are absolutely important for the safety of the players and a big enough reason to change venues, the option to upgrade the pitch to a hybrid model was shut down by none other than New York Red Bulls due to a friendly game—AKA a game that does not count towards their season—they have on Saturday. Although the pitch would be ready for Gotham FC’s big game, RBNY shut down the plan as it wouldn’t be ready for their planned friendly. All of a sudden, a game-making match for a winning women’s team was being moved to a much smaller space due to a men’s game that does not count towards anything.

If you’re getting a sense of deja vu, that’s because this is not the first time a women’s team had to change their plans due to a men’s team. The WNBA’s Atlanta Dream were forced to move one of their WNBA Finals games from 8 p.m. to 12:30 p.m. on a Thursday. While the move was caused by them switching venues—going from their home at the Gateway Center, which fits 3,500 to the State Farm Arena which seats 17,000—it was also caused by a scheduled Atlanta Hawks game, a team that hasn’t won an NBA title since the 1950s.

It’s clear that no matter where they go, these women’s teams’ homes are not giving them what they need: a space where they can continue to host their ever-growing fanbases without fear of having to fall to the needs of a men’s team. The WNBA had a 21 percent increase in attendance to their games in 2026. The NWSL also gained some new faces in the crowd with a 3 percent increase, according to Sports Business Journal.

In 2028, Gotham FC will move to Etihad Park, a new stadium that will hold 25,000 fans in Queens, NYC. However, they’ll still have the problem of sharing the field with New York City FC, meaning this problem can always come up again.

A clear solution would be to give these incredible teams their own venues, but it’s evident that, financially, building a new stadium is not the easiest for some of these teams who are still growing. But, maybe next time, these men’s teams will just get the hint and let the real winners take over. It’s their home, too, after all.

#6745. Stop Hunting for Pricey Antiques—Designers Say These 9 Humble Finds Give a Home So Much Character

They’re affordable, widely available, and stylish.

It’s any avid thrifter’s dream to stumble upon an extremely valuable piece of decor while browsing the shelves of their favorite antique store. But while you’re hunting for rare treasures, don’t discount the less-expensive finds—they can be the secret to a home that looks instantly filled with character.

“To me, a collected home is much more interesting when everything isn’t perfect or chosen for its monetary value,” designer Lauren Sullivan of Well by Design tells House Beautiful. “Often, it’s the less precious pieces that give a room its personality.”

Even as older generations are continuing to downsize and offload unwanted things to local thrift stores, it still might be hard to find the diamonds in the rough. Keep reading to find out what Sullivan, and a handful of other designers, say you should be on the lookout for on your next thrifting visit.

The most valuable antiques are the ones that have been passed down through generations. In Sullivan’s own bathroom, the designer keeps her great-grandmother’s vanity set on the countertop, its aged finish providing beautiful contrast with the more contemporary marble and finishes. In her bedroom, the same great-grandmother’s small bedside clock found a place on Sullivan’s nightstand.

“Neither is particularly valuable monetarily, but I love incorporating pieces that have been passed down through generations into a home filled with both old and new,” Sullivan says.

Designers love incorporating artwork into homes to add personality and dimension, but Sullivan, as well as designer Becca Meyer of BB Meyer Design, believe that antique shops are where you can really find some hidden gems.

“Some of my favorite pieces of art have come from flea markets or antique shops for very little money,” Meyer says. “I much prefer these to mass produced prints.” You’re more likely to find pieces with aged textures at these types of shops, and that patinaed quality creates an entirely unique look.

Sullivan explains that she’s actually particularly drawn to prints, as well as smaller paintings and silhouettes. She even has an inexpensive print of The Skating Minister in a vintage frame in one of her guest rooms, pictured above. “These smaller, or sometimes quirky, pieces can bring so much personality to a space without requiring a significant investment,” she adds. They may not be originals, but they’re definitely unique.

Old pottery and ceramics are widely available, and they are usually affordable as well. Designer Brooke Snyder of Design Hutch loves how they make any place feel more curated.

The imperfections these handmade pieces carry, from uneven shapes, variations in glaze, or evidence of years of use, are what help these items bring a warmth to a room, particularly when placed near more refined materials, according to Sullivan. They feel humble, maybe even utilitarian, especially when displayed in a kitchen. Or, better yet, used in a kitchen (as long as they’re safe).

“They're beautiful on their own but also so functional for holding utensils or branches or flowers,” Meyer adds. “They make any space feel a little more lived-in and loved and help tell a story—and they don't break the bank!”

“I love incorporating vintage plates, old copper pots, and woven baskets into a home,” explains designer Joe Waroquier. Though these are run-of-the-mill, everyday objects, they can take on and provide an entirely different life when displayed in the home as decor. Mismatched plates on a wall, a collection of copper pots hanging on a rod or resting on shelves in the kitchen, an old crock filled with branches—these items invite in warmth, texture, and more personality.

“I’m particularly drawn to pieces that show their age, whether it’s a little patina, a worn finish, or an imperfect glaze,” he adds. “I love mixing these finds with more contemporary furnishings because that contrast makes a room feel collected and personal rather than overly designed.”

Mirrors can be intriguing focal points of a room, or they can add dimension to a gallery wall. However you want to style them, Snyder loves finding these reflective pieces at antique malls and vintage shops.

“Purchasing something that you don’t see every day from a retailer allows this unique piece to stand out on its own, without feeling like something you see everywhere or feels too simple,” she explains.

As Snyder puts it, antique table lamps provide high visual impact with low investment. Adding one to your accent table or on a bookshelf is an easy way to make your space feel more curated while inviting in another level of ambience with the lighting.

These vintage lamps in the above space, for instance, were bought from a collector and make the primary bedroom feel much more charming and curated. And unlike antique sconces, there’s no need to hardwire these into the wall.

Besides pottery and lamps, designer Ryann Swan Hackett of Ryann Swan Design loves to find and collect other small tabletop decor for her projects. Think candleholders, vintage coffee table books, and trays—things that can layer a little history and individuality into a room or simple vignette.

“If you’re lucky,” Swan Hackett says of the vintage coffee table books, “you’ll find an old inscription inside, which gives the book a story of its own.”

In terms of midsize accent pieces, Meyer says that she has a soft spot for finding antique stools “because you can pop them into any space to add depth and dimension.” Tuck them beside an armchair in the living room, add them to an empty kitchen corner and place a plant on top, or stick one in a bathroom by the tub for somewhere to keep a drink while relaxing. And if you find an upholstered one that could use new fabric, switching it out for a pattern you love is probably worth it.

“These bring a natural warmth that can be difficult to replicate with something new, and their small size makes them fairly reasonable in price for a vintage furnishing piece,” Meyer says.

A larger piece of furniture is likely always going to be more expensive than a smaller one, but designer Yena Jung of By Yena Designs thinks vintage hutches are the versatile antiques people should pay more attention to. They come in many different shapes, styles, and sizes, and can easily make a home in any room of the house.

“A beautiful vintage hutch can add warmth, charm, and a sense of history to a space,” Jung says. “I especially love how their imperfections and natural patina make them feel collected over time.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6738. Everything We Know About ‘The Body’ Season 2

Need more of The Body? The Netflix series is off to an excellent start, with fresh new characters and spooky, sexy situations that really take some turns. Here’s what we know about whether or not The Body has been renewed for season 2.

Set at a Catholic high school in the so-called innocence of 2004, the first season of The Body is about two girls who can’t express their feelings for each other, an ambitious dance team heading to regionals, and a mass hysteria that leads to prophetic visions and Satanic rituals. Totes normal, right? Once you’ve sped through season 1, you’ll be on the edge of your seat waiting to see what happens next.

As of the season 1 drop, The Body has not yet been renewed for season 2. It’s still early, however, and the buzz around the show has been good. Fingers crossed!

The Body is an original idea from Quinn Shepherd, not based on any pre-existing IP. So what happens next is entirely up to her and her team of writers and producers. Luckily, the first season ends with a cliff-hanger and a lot of unanswered questions.

“We always knew that that was where the show was going, Shepherd said in a post-mortem interview with Deadline about *that* thing with Grace. “From the beginning, that was the arc of the first season, and I think a lot of that has to do with things we would love to do next season and how to keep carrying the story.”

Executive producer Gina Gammell also mentioned Anya confessing to Father Franklin, Maddie’s prophecy, and the mystery over whether Tyler and Lisbeth actually slept together as potential avenues for season 2.

Given that the show doesn’t have a lot of movie stars with busy schedules in major roles, and filming on season 1 took place in 2025, it stands to reason that The Body could easily return in late 2027. But sometimes Netflix shows take a little longer. So again... cross your fingers and do everything short of ritual sacrifice to ensure The Body comes back for season 2 as soon as possible.

#6733. 10 Minimalist Thanksgiving Manicures That Are Perfect for the Holiday—How to Recreate

Celebrity nail artists share their favorite chic yet simple looks.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

First and foremost, Thanksgiving is about spending time with family, and then, eating all the food you can possibly ingest in the stretchiest pair of pants you own. (The two priorities can be slightly interchangeable.) And while you’re planning your outfit for the holiday, why not have a chic manicure to match? I mean, your fingers will be the ones at center stage holding a fork all night.

If you don’t want to go all out with adorned turkeys and food-themed designs, there are plenty of Thanksgiving manicure ideas that are equal parts chic and on-theme. “Minimalist doesn’t have to mean forgettable,” says celebrity nail artist Max Estrada. “Sometimes a beautiful neutral, a little sparkle, or the perfect French tip says everything.” Below, I chatted with manicurists for their favorite nail looks for the holiday feast.

Keep scrolling minimalist Thanksgiving nail ideas, from earthy polishes and elevated designs that will look gorgeous well into the end of November.

“For those who prefer a minimalist look, I’d recommend a classic French manicure with a seasonal twist, such as chocolate brown tips on a sheer nude base,” says celebrity nail artist Vikki Swan.

“Another beautiful option is using different shades from the same color family across the nails, transitioning from lighter to deeper tones,” Swan explains. “It’s a simple yet elegant way to capture the fall mood without intricate nail art.” This easy gradient requires no intricate art skills, and brings the fall foliage to your fingtertips.

“Cat-eye effects give those rich colors movement and dimension, so the manicure becomes interesting through the finish itself,” says Estrada. “The color and light are doing the work, and make a statement without adding any other nail art.” All it takes is your favorite shifting polish and a magnet to get the look.

Polka dots are yes, chic, but more importantly super easy to DIY if you still want to lean into a low-effort design. Choose your favorite contrasting autumnal hues and use a dotting tool (or even a toothpick) in the lighter polish to create the look. Or go for the reverse finish, by using the lighter color as the base.

Burgundy French tips are another favorite minimalist option of Swan’s, and adding a cat-eye effect gives some dimension to the classic yet seasonal manicure.

“Deep burgundy and wine red are also beautiful seasonal choices,” Swan says. “These colors complement fall wardrobes perfectly and create that cozy autumn feeling.” Some of our favorite quintessential fall reds include essie’s Bordeaux (how fitting!) and Sally Hansen’s Pomp & Plum.

“During this time of year, warm, rich shades are especially popular, including chocolate brown, espresso, and caramel,” says Swan. To get the coffee-esque hue, try OPI’s Espresso Your Inner Self.

If you want to lean into the theme of the feast, go for this subtle pumpkin pie-coded manicure. Choose two shades of orange and create a light-to dark gradient by blending the hues outwards with a small sponge.

These translucent jelly nails have the same decadent finish as a sweet Thanksgiving treat. To get the look, apply a sheer coat or two of your favorite polish, then seal it all in with a high-shine top coat for that signature glassy effect.

Pumpkin spice season goes well into November, and this reflective manicure is proof. Start with an orange base, then layer on a silver or orange chrome topper for a metallic finish that feels festive but still wearable well beyond the big feast.

Jasmine Hyman is the assistant beauty editor at Cosmo who covers all things nails—from the latest design trends to new polish launches. For this story, she consulted celebrity nail artists for their favorite minimalist Thanksgiving nail ideas.

Jasmine Hyman is the Assistant Beauty Editor at Cosmopolitan, where she writes about the latest beauty trends and must-have products. Her most prized beauty possessions are a meticulous skincare routine and salon blowouts. You’ll also likely find her in bed reading a good book or endlessly scrolling TikTok (spoiler: it’s usually the latter) while listening to Harry Styles’ entire discography on repeat. Follow her on Insta to be inundated with pictures of her meals.

#6732. This Colorful South Carolina Home Marries Mediterranean Elegance With Lowcountry Charm

Designer Megan Molten worked handmade details and custom elements into every room.

How do you blend the magical ease of Mediterranean life with the coastal charm of South Carolina’s lowcountry? After an enchanting vacation in Greece with their three daughters, Megan Molten’s clients wanted their new build on Daniel Island to capture that unique, transportive feeling they fell in love with—and they asked the Charleston-based designer to help them achieve it.

Alongside builder Wes Whittle of EasTech Property Development, Molten was involved from the very beginning, crafting the blank canvas into a residence that balanced the sun-drenched elegance of the Greek islands with traditional Lowcountry living. The designer went all in on every detail, relying on high-quality craftsmanship, custom pieces, and handmade elements to bring life to every room.

Upon entry, guests are greeted by a hand-painted, botanical-themed mural by local artist Jenn Griffith. “The mural’s objective was very specific,” says Molten. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry.” Turn to the other side and find a plaster-covered stairwell by artist Emily Pope Harris, an addition that sets the tone for the whole home. Move further into the home and find a hand-painted floor that shines in the craft room, created using a stencil from Etsy and a few Sherwin-Williams paint colors—it’s playfully fabulous and still somehow timeless. “Clients do not typically splurge in rooms that are not front and center, so this was a real treat to bring to life, especially knowing how much their daughters love to craft,” the designer adds.

Elsewhere, a larger portion of the budget was split between the lighting and the kitchen. “Statement lighting in the house was always prioritized,” Molten says. “The homeowners also agreed that the lighting was important and needed to be special. I was finally able to use some light fixtures that I have been eyeing for years!”

As for the kitchen, it was more difficult to know where to splurge and where to save, a predicament the designer finds herself with on most projects. However, looking now at the honed limestone backsplash, green quartzite countertops with an integrated sink, and a plaster hood by Pope Harris, it’s clear that she made the right choices.

Even with all the home’s beautiful elements, the fan-favorite among Molten’s team ended up being the pool just outside the kitchen. It’s lower than most Molten has worked on in Charleston, making it one of the most unique she’s seen. Flanked by archways and paired with two spas and a raised hot tub, it fully captures a Grecian grotto feel.

Even with all the one-of-a-kind features and elevated furnishings, the home is a place where life is meant to be experienced to the fullest. “The spaces are to be enjoyed without hesitation,” Molten explains, “creating a beautiful, practical environment for a life well lived.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Megan MoltenArchitect: DLB Custom Home DesignBuilder: EasTech Property DevelopmentLocation: Daniel Island, South CarolinaThe Space: A six-bedroom, seven-bathroom new build spanning 6,000 square feet.

The beauty of this entryway starts outside with the front door. Made locally by Maebeck Doors, it’s an arched shape with a grid pattern that serves as a charming “wow” moment upon entering the home.

Once inside the house, the awe continues, from the curved plaster stairwell to the custom botanical mural by artist Jenn Griffith. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry,” Molten adds. The final touch? A stone table in the notch at the base of the stairs, perfect for placing keys or bags at the end of the day.

Molten’s client wanted a large, comfortable sectional that could easily seat her family and any guests, so when the team saw these two at High Point Market, they knew they were perfect for the space. A travertine table, nestled between the two sectionals, made the pieces feel like one long, continuous design, and both were upholstered in Crypton performance fabric for added durability.

To complement the space, Molten added a plush armchair and topped the room off with a modern light fixture that doesn’t add much noise. “The artwork above the sectional is from the homeowner’s own collection,” Molten says. “It served as the catalyst for the colorways used throughout the home.”

A calming, functional space, the brightness of the kitchen is achieved not through a classic all-white kitchen, but through shades of light green, from the quartzite countertops to the greenish-gray cabinets. Rattan pendant lights bring in that casual Mediterranean feel, especially since the floor-to-ceiling windows on the left overlook the pool below.

To keep it family-friendly, Molten applied one of her favorite tricks: leather on the counter stool seats and fabric on the back. “Wiping off messes and letting the leather patina is both practical and pretty,” she adds.

This dining nook really needed to be functional, since it’s where Molten’s clients and their three children sit for breakfast every morning and dinner every night. Durability for daily use played a huge part in the furniture choices; they sourced a long wooden table and used the same seating upholstery trick here as they did with the counter stools in the kitchen. For a touch of whimsy, Molten crowned the nook with a coco bead chandelier.

Instead of plaster on the double-sided fireplace as she had initially intended, Molten went with a zellige tile façade that produces a lovely shine.

Easily accessible from the living room, kitchen, and screened-in porch, this breakfast area—complete with a full bar—is perfect for hosting. Anyone can come in and make themselves a drink whenever they want.

Arched upper cabinets with lattice details create more movement in this space, as well as add in charm, and the chandelier has been on the designer’s list of favorites for a while. “I particularly love the contrast of the black countertop with the coral cabinets,” Molten says.

The first thing Molten selected for the scullery was the charming lattice window over the sink looking out onto the yard. Then came the bold wallpaper, which inspired the vibrant cabinet paint color, balanced out with a light Calacatta marble countertop with hints of yellow and gold throughout.

“This scullery is a very functional space that houses their microwave, coffee maker, refrigerator, and drawers with snacks, all built-in and panel-ready,” Molten says.

When her clients told her they wanted a dedicated craft room for their girls, Molten was just as excited at the prospect as their daughters. “A dedicated specialty room like this typically provides a great opportunity for lots of creativity and color,” she explains.

They started with the colorful striped wallpaper on the ceiling, pulling colors from it for the built-in cubby shelf and the painted floors. A neutral light fixture keeps the room from feeling too busy, giving it a more timeless feel. “The floor design is so special, and a bit of a splurge,” Molten adds. “I was so pleased the client embraced this idea!”

The unique wallpaper served as the starting point for this powder bath, as Molten designed everything else around it. “The Jamie Young mirror looks like an oyster shell, and the floating stone was a nice feature,” she says. “Painting the trim and ceiling the pink shell color made the wallpaper, tile, and stone come together.”

Molten had never done a wallpaper border before, but she adores how it turned out in one of the daughter’s bedrooms. “It’s a great way to add thoughtful design details to a room and is cost-effective too, as opposed to covering the full room in wallpaper,” she says. The trim color was pulled from this border, which she paired with a signature shade of pink from her own collection with Sherwin-Williams.

“For the daughter’s bathroom, we opted for a tub/shower combo to accommodate young children,” Molten explains. These children-friendly details are found elsewhere in the room too, like a pull-out step stool under the sink cabinets.

The pink-and-green palette from the daughter’s bedroom is continued here, though in slightly more saturated shades. A local Charleston brand, Mirth Studio, created the painted wood valance to pair with the Pepper Home shower curtain, and Pope Harris continued her plaster work here, covering a Visual Comfort & Co. sconce over the mirror in a custom pink hue.

Located on the small third floor, the only rooms that occupy this level are the office, the office den, and a guest suite, making it a quiet place to escape when the clients, who share this space, need to get work done. “Our client loved the Tiger Palm wallpaper, so we applied that on all of the walls and at the low part of the angled ceiling to help mask all of the ceiling lines in this room,” Molten says. They even have their own wet bar up here for post-work happy hour.

Designed as a dedicated no-kids zone off the office, this private room is where Molten’s clients get away to watch movies after bedtime, decompress after a long day, and just retreat to when they need time to themselves.

“We opted for a performance velvet on the sofa with velvet textured pillows to create a luxurious, cozy feel,” the designer says. “I love the Currey & Co. chandelier with green glass—it works so perfectly in this small room.”

“Like the primary bedroom and primary bathroom, the closet is not huge, but it’s efficient and thoughtfully designed,” Molten explains. With her team, they designed a wall of built-ins and paid close attention to the functionality of each drawer and shelf while maximizing the space; then a local cabinet maker brought them to life.

Molten chose soft, luxurious carpet that feels like stepping into a high-end department store and a fabulous Made Goods light fixture.

The architect who worked on this house, Damien Busillo of DLB Custom Home Design, had this lower-level pool incorporated in the original plans, and Molten was able to assist with the tile selections.

“This is such a creative way to use empty space under the house,” she says. “Homes in Charleston are often raised when built due to coastal flooding; however, this was the most interesting and exciting design concept I have seen for this area of the home.” It truly captures that Grecian grotto vibe.

Megan Molten has always been creative, but her love of interiors didn’t fully manifest until she bought and decorated her first house in Charleston, South Carolina. From there, she started experimenting with design as a hobby, sharing her eye with friends and family, before devoting all her time to it and finally deciding to open her own eponymous firm. Now, she shares her elevated coastal style and luxurious Lowcountry vibe with clients across the country.

#6730. These Cozy Shoes Are Too Chic to Keep Indoors

Slippers in public? These fashionable pairs make a convincing case.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

At this point, we’ve all probably accepted that slippers are no longer strictly house shoes. They’ve been part of our everyday wardrobes for years, and it’s hardly unusual to see someone wearing a fuzzy pair of slip-ons with jeans and a nice coat. What’s more interesting is how far brands have taken the idea lately. And honestly, some of these new styles are just really good shoes, slippers or not.

Take Ugg’s Western-inspired designs. Cowboy boots aren’t going anywhere, and the brand has found a way to bring that look into the comfortable footwear it’s known for. It’s a fun alternative to a traditional Western boot, especially if you want something a little more relaxed for everyday wear.

Brunch’s collaboration with Madhappy is another one worth knowing about. The two brands share co-founder Joshua Sitt, so the partnership makes sense, but the shoes themselves are what caught our attention. Made with colorful crochet and lined with shearling, they look like someone turned a chunky knit sweater into a slipper. And considering how much we love a good sweater this time of year, why shouldn’t our feet get a sweater of their own?

For Sitt, the idea of making slippers part of an everyday wardrobe has always been central to Brunch. “Originally, the entire concept for the brand was taking the hotel slipper product which is famously associated with vacation and travel, and bringing it into something you can wear every single day inside and outside of your home,” he tells ELLE.

You can see that thinking at work in the Madhappy collaboration. “The shoe itself morphs the comfort that both brands prioritize as well as the fashion pieces of both brands,” he says.

What’s particularly interesting is that this isn’t limited to brands you’d normally associate with slippers. Hunter, whose rain boots have been a wardrobe staple for years, has a cozy option of its own. Simkhai has entered the category, too, and Alo has a pair that feels especially fitting for the Pilates-to-coffee crowd. It’s a pretty good indication of just how much room there is to play with a shoe that used to be purely practical.

The styling is arguably the best part. A Western-inspired pair would look great with straight-leg jeans and a suede jacket, while a softer, more understated slipper can make tailored trousers and a wool coat feel less formal. You don’t need to rethink your wardrobe to wear them. In fact, that’s part of the appeal.

As Sitt points out, slippers haven’t exactly been the most considered purchase in our wardrobes. Ask people what pair they own, and “you’ll get some crickets,” he says. Or maybe they “stole a pair at a wedding.” No offense to wedding favors, but we think it’s time for an upgrade.

Whether you’re looking for an alternative to your usual boots or simply want a pair of slippers you won’t want to change out of before leaving the house, these are the styles worth a closer look. Shop our favorites below.

#6723. What Does Transgender Mean? Everything You Need to Know, According to Experts

From gender identity to transition, here's what the term actually means.

The word "transgender" gets used a lot these days, but that doesn't necessarily indicate that everyone understands what being transgender actually means. Between headlines, social media debates, and ongoing conversations about gender identity, it's easy to feel like you're missing a piece of the puzzle or even worry about using the wrong terminology altogether. Fortunately, understanding what it means to be transgender doesn't have to be as complicated as the internet sometimes makes it seem.

"Being transgender means your gender identity doesn't align with the one you were assigned at birth. That's the whole heart of it," says licensed marriage and family therapist Adrienne Michelle, LMFT. "It doesn't tell you anything about how someone looks, what they've explored medically, or how far along they are in any journey. It's simply about your authentic sense of self being different from what you were told you were at the start."

Ultimately, gender is deeply personal, and there's no one "right" way to be transgender. Whether you're talking with a friend, supporting a loved one, or simply trying to better understand the world around you, having a basic understanding of transgender identity can make the conversation feel a whole lot less intimidating and a lot more inclusive.

The term "transgender" is an identity label that refers to people whose gender identity doesn’t match the sex they were assigned when they were born, explains therapist and LGBTQ+ educator Zoe Stoller. You'll also often hear transgender shortened to simply trans. The prefix trans- means "across" or "on the other side of," reflecting that a person's gender identity differs from the sex they were assigned at birth.

"Gender is expansive, and 'trans' is the umbrella term that often holds all of that expansiveness," says Michelle. "It isn't a single identity; it's a beautifully wide category that makes room for so many people."

The transgender umbrella includes trans men, trans women, and many nonbinary identities, though not everyone whose gender identity differs from the sex they were assigned at birth identifies as transgender. And that's completely okay. The right label is the one that feels right to the person using it.

Although they're often used interchangeably, sex and gender aren't the same thing. Sex refers to the biological characteristics someone is labeled or assigned at birth, while gender refers to a person's internal sense of who they are. You may also come across the terms AFAB/AMAB (assigned female at birth/assigned male at birth), which describe the sex someone was assigned when they were born, not their gender identity. And while babies are often labeled male or female at birth, sex itself isn't always binary, as intersex people exist too.

Once you understand that distinction, a lot of the terminology starts to make more sense. Terms like nonbinary, genderfluid, genderqueer, and gender nonconforming all describe distinct experiences or expressions of gender, but they aren't interchangeable, and they don't all automatically mean someone is transgender. (P.S. If you're looking for a deeper breakdown of those identities, check out our complete gender identity glossary.)

"Gender nonconforming is about not feeling aligned with the normative, performative ways gender is 'supposed' to show up—the expectations around how a man or woman should look, dress, or move through the world," says Michelle. "It's about expression, not identity. You can be gender nonconforming and cisgender, and you can be gender nonconforming and trans. The two sometimes overlap, but they're answering different questions: transgender is about who you are, gender nonconforming is about how you show up."

As Stoller explains, the transgender umbrella includes both binary identities (such as trans men and trans women) and many nonbinary identities, though not everyone whose gender differs from the sex they were assigned at birth identifies as transgender. Like every other aspect of identity, everyone's relationship with gender is unique.

First things first: Despite the headline right above this, there's no one way to "look" transgender.

As Stoller explains, being transgender isn't defined by surgeries, medical procedures, or any other external milestone. The only thing someone needs to "count" as transgender is feeling that the label accurately reflects who they are. For many people, that identity is deeply affirming regardless of whether they ever choose to transition.

Transitioning simply refers to the process of aligning someone's life more closely with their gender identity. "It’s important to note that transition isn't a checklist," says Michelle. "Some people explore hormones or surgery, some focus on legal or social changes, some pursue none of it, and every one of those paths is whole and valid."

Transitioning can look different for everyone, but it's generally broken into a few categories:

Some people choose to align their outward appearance with their gender identity because it's an affirming—and sometimes life-saving—way to feel seen as their authentic selves, Stoller says. Others don't transition at all or may be unable to do so for personal, financial, or safety reasons. Regardless, their gender identity is no less valid.

Now that you know what transgender means, it's worth talking about how to actually use the term. According to Michelle, "transgender" is an adjective, not a noun. "You'd say 'a transgender person' or 'transgender people,' never 'a transgender' or 'the transgenders,'" she explains. "It works just like any other descriptive word; you wouldn't call someone 'a tall,' so the same care applies here."

As Stoller explains, it's also important not to assume someone identifies as transgender unless they've shared that information with you. Even if you think someone's gender identity technically falls under the transgender umbrella, it's ultimately up to them to decide which labels feel the most accurate and affirming. When in doubt, use the language someone uses to describe themselves.

One of the most common terminology mix-ups is the difference between a trans man and a trans woman. "A trans man is someone who was assigned female at birth and now identifies and lives as a man. A trans woman was assigned male at birth and identifies and lives as a woman," explains Michelle.

It's an important distinction because these terms describe who someone is, not who they used to be. In other words, trans men are men, and trans women are women. The word trans simply provides additional context about their gender journey—it doesn't change their gender.

As Stoller notes, some trans men and trans women identify fully within the traditional gender binary, while others may also feel connected to nonbinary experiences. Like every other aspect of gender identity, there's no one "right" way to experience or describe being transgender.

The short answer? Use the pronouns someone asks you to use. It's really that simple.

As Stoller explains, people—whether they're transgender or cisgender—can use whatever pronouns feel the most affirming to them, including multiple sets of pronouns or neopronouns. Although people often associate certain pronouns with certain genders, pronouns don't always tell you someone's gender identity.

If you aren't sure which pronouns someone uses, it's okay to politely ask. And if you accidentally get it wrong, apologize, correct yourself, and move on.

Another common misconception is that being transgender somehow determines who someone is attracted to. It doesn't.

Gender identity and sexual orientation are two completely different things (and both are different from romantic orientation), Michelle explains. Gender identity describes who someone is, while sexual orientation describes who they're attracted to. That means transgender people can be straight, gay, lesbian, bisexual, pansexual, asexual, or identify with any other sexual orientation—just like cisgender people.

Like gender identity, sexuality is deeply personal. Labels can be helpful, Stoller says, but they don't always capture the full picture of someone's experience, so it's always best to use the language someone chooses for themselves.

Being a good LGBTQ ally doesn't require having all the answers. It starts with listening, staying open to learning, and treating transgender people with the same respect you'd want someone to show you.

Use the name and pronouns someone asks you to use, and don't make assumptions based on their appearance or gender identity. As Michelle explains, a trans man might use he/him pronouns, a trans woman might use she/her, and a nonbinary person might use they/them—or another set of pronouns that feels most affirming to them. If you aren't sure, it's okay to politely ask.

You may also come across the word transsexual, particularly in older books, articles, or medical literature. While some transgender people have reclaimed the term for themselves, Stoller notes that it's generally considered more outdated and medicalized than transgender, and many people find it offensive. Unless someone has specifically told you that's the label they use, it's best to avoid it.

Above all, remember that every transgender person's experience is different. Listening, respecting the language people use to describe themselves, and remaining open to learning will go a long way.

If you've made it this far because you're wondering whether the word transgender might describe you, know that you don't have to have everything figured out today. Gender identity isn't a quiz you pass or fail, and there's no deadline for understanding yourself.

As Stoller explains, there's no single way to experience being transgender because every person's relationship with gender is different. Some people transition socially, medically, or legally, while others never transition at all. None of those experiences make someone's identity any more or less real.

If you'd like support, consider talking with an LGBTQIA+-affirming therapist, connecting with trusted friends or loved ones, or exploring resources from organizations like The Trevor Project can be a good place to start. You don't have to navigate questions about your identity alone.

As Michelle puts it, "Gender is expansive, full stop." The most important thing isn't fitting neatly into someone else's definition; it's giving yourself the space to discover what feels authentic to you.

#6721. The Benefits of Sauna Therapy: What to Know Before You Sweat

Experts explain how sauna sessions may support circulation, recovery, skin health, and stress relief.

Although saunas may not be the most relaxing spa or high-end gym amenity, recent studies show that as little as 30 minutes in a hot box can significantly boost the immune system and reduce the risk of cardiovascular and mental health issues, including hypertension and dementia. A physician should monitor the practice, particularly if you have any preexisting conditions. Sauna sessions can also offer several beauty benefits. The research reveals that quick stints in high heat are just enough to jump-start stress reduction, help fight off a cold, and even give skin a nice little glow-up. Experts break down the perks of sauna therapy and how to do it safely.

According to board-certified surgeon, physician, and longevity specialist Dr. Darshan Shah, who is also the founder and CEO of Next Health, sauna therapy offers several potential benefits for both physical and mental well-being, including:

Muscle Recovery: Increased blood flow helps deliver oxygen and nutrients to muscle tissue, supporting recovery and relieving muscle tension.

Skin Rejuvenation: Increased circulation helps deliver oxygen and nutrients to the skin, supporting a healthy-looking complexion. Heat exposure may also support skin health and collagen production, which plays an important role in maintaining skin elasticity and firmness.

Immunity & Cardiovascular Health: Heat exposure raises core body temperature, supporting the body’s natural immune response, while promoting vasodilation and improved circulation. Regular sauna use may also help support healthy blood pressure and vascular function.

Weight Management: Heat exposure temporarily increases heart rate and energy expenditure—although he clarifies that sweating primarily results in water loss rather than fat loss.

Detoxification: Sweating helps the body eliminate small amounts of certain substances, while heat exposure may also support circulation and lymphatic function.

There’s a difference, but it’s worth noting that the aforementioned study and conclusions gathered from it focused on dry heat. However, Shah says the main difference is how the heat is delivered and how your body responds to each.

Brianna Olamiju, a board-certified dermatologist at Spring Street Dermatology in NYC, adds that the benefits of dry saunas and steam rooms are similar, including increased circulation and relaxation. “Dry saunas typically operate at higher temperatures with lower humidity, while steam rooms have lower temperatures and significantly higher humidity,” she explains. Steam rooms may temporarily relieve nasal congestion, but Olamiju shares that neither has been proven superior for skin health. “People with sensitive skin, eczema, or rosacea may find that excessive heat aggravates their symptoms,” she tells ELLE.

Shah’s take is that ultimately, the best option depends on personal preference, heat tolerance, and individual wellness goals. “Consistency and finding an approach that feels comfortable are more important than choosing one type of sauna over another.”

Traditional dry saunas typically operate between 150°F and 195°F, while steam rooms are generally around 100°F to 120°F. Olamiju explains that although many studies have examined sauna benefits at approximately 175°F, she says there is no universally established ideal temperature. “I recommend starting at a comfortable temperature and gradually increasing as tolerated. Higher temperatures don’t necessarily translate to greater benefits,” says Olamiju.

Temperature matters when determining the intensity of heat exposure and how your body responds. Shah concurs that hotter doesn’t necessarily mean better. “The goal is to find a temperature that allows you to experience the benefits of heat exposure without putting unnecessary stress on your body,” he adds.

Another option that Shah’s Next Health saunas utilize is infrared heat. These chambers operate between 110°F and 140°F. “Unlike traditional saunas, which can reach significantly higher temperatures, infrared technology uses light-based heat to warm the body directly. This creates a more tolerable experience for many individuals and can allow for longer sessions,” Shah explains.

Studies show that dry air at high temperatures (typically 70 to 100 °C) for 10 to 30 minutes per session was the sweet spot to reap some of the more significant benefits of sauna therapy, but Shah says there is no one-size-fits-all number since the frequency of infrared sauna use will vary depending on individual factors such as health goals, tolerance, and time constraints. “As a starting point, I generally recommend a client aim for two to three sessions per week for maximized benefits,” says Shah.

For beginners, Olamiju suggests starting with five to 10 minutes per session and gradually increasing to 15 to 20 minutes as tolerated. Two to three sessions per week are recommended as a reasonable starting point. “Some studies suggest that more frequent sauna use may be associated with greater cardiovascular benefits, but consistency and individual tolerance are most important,” Olamiju also notes.

Both experts agree that a wide range of individuals can benefit. For Olamiju, it’s anyone looking to reduce stress, improve relaxation, or relieve muscle soreness. “It may also offer cardiovascular benefits for some individuals. However, sauna therapy should complement, rather than replace, regular exercise and other healthy lifestyle habits,” she advises.

Shah says sauna therapy is particularly useful for those looking to support circulation, recovery, skin health, and overall well-being. He adds that if you’re experiencing chronic pain or muscle tension, a sauna session can help, as increased blood flow may help alleviate discomfort and support recovery. Lastly, he says sauna therapy may also support skin health by promoting collagen production, which can improve skin elasticity and contribute to a smoother, more radiant complexion. “Overall, sauna therapy may be particularly appealing to individuals looking to manage everyday aches and pains, support skin rejuvenation, improve circulation, or simply incorporate more relaxation and recovery into their wellness routines,” says Shah.

Like any form of heat therapy, sauna use isn’t appropriate for everyone, and it’s important to understand your individual health considerations. “Individuals with cardiovascular conditions, uncontrolled blood pressure, a history of fainting, or other underlying health concerns should consult their physician before starting sauna therapy. Pregnant individuals should also seek medical guidance before using a sauna,” Shah warns.

Some of the biggest risks of sauna therapy are dehydration, overheating, dizziness, and low blood pressure, so stay hydrated before, during, and after your sessions. From a dermatologic perspective, Olamiju also explains that excessive heat and sweating can exacerbate conditions like rosacea, eczema, and sensitive skin. “I recommend staying hydrated, rinsing off after sauna sessions, and applying moisturizer to support the skin barrier,” she says.

Can older individuals reap the benefits of sauna therapy?

Absolutely. But both Shah and Olamiju warn that age can bring changes in cardiovascular function, blood pressure regulation, and the body’s ability to manage heat. If you’re taking medications that affect blood pressure or hydration, consult your physician before beginning sauna therapy. Also, start with shorter sessions at lower temperatures and gradually adjust based on comfort and tolerance. “The goal isn’t to achieve a particular temperature or duration; it’s to find a safe, sustainable routine that supports long-term health and quality of life,” says Shah.

#6719. You're Shopping for Mattresses All Wrong—an Expert Says You Need to Try the "One-Roll" Test

It’s the best way to mimic how you’d actually use it at home.

Buying a mattress is not an easy decision, especially if you want a change from your current model or you’re upgrading to a different style to address aches and pains. The process feels high-stakes, which is why our editors are enthusiastic about testing and recommending mattresses, plus sharing any tips and tricks we learn along the way to prevent buyer’s remorse. Recently, we learned that picking out a bed simply based on its designated labels—twin, full, queen, king, and Cal king—may not be the most efficient way to buy a mattress.

Instead, Paul Keenan, store manager at U.K.-based brand Linthorpe Beds, suggested several sleep tests to gain more insight into buying the perfect mattress for your needs. “Most people know whether they sleep on a [full, queen or king-size mattress], but far fewer have ever checked whether those dimensions actually suit their body, sleeping habits and bedroom,” he notes. “Bed size is really easy to overlook because most of us shop using familiar labels like queen or king rather than thinking about the actual measurements we need.”

He goes on to suggest that your height, how much you move at night, and whether you share the mattress with someone else can all affect how much space feels comfortable. “There are some very simple checks you can carry out at home before deciding whether your current setup is right for you,” he says. Read ahead to try out his easy “tests” before you buy your next mattress.

This is a test you can try in stores if you’re testing the mattresses there, or at home if you’re doing a trial run with the mattress you just bought. These days, most brands offer at least 30 days of testing to see if you like the bed. To start, position yourself in the part of your bed you’d normally sleep on. Then, roll naturally into another comfortable position, such as from your back onto your side.

“Don’t deliberately tuck your arms or legs in to make yourself fit; the aim is simply to recreate the sort of movement you would make during the night,” says Keenan. “We move around far more in our sleep than we realize, so whether you can simply lie on a mattress isn’t the only thing to think about.

Most people, when testing a mattress in stores, just plop down horizontally and get up versus tossing and turning. The key is to shift your whole body into different positions, ideally with your partner next to you, to see if you both fit on the bed. If you sleep alone, you’re in the clear.

“If one normal turn takes you straight to the edge or into the person next to you, that can be a useful sign that the amount of usable space is more restricted than it feels when you’re lying still,” Keenan says. “It isn’t a strict pass or fail test, but it gives you another reason to look at the actual width you have available [versus relying on the categories of full, queen, king, etc].”

This one-roll test is helpful because, while mattress sizes are standardized, the physical size of two people sharing the bed changes how spacious a mattress can feel. Thus, what works for one couple may not work for another, so the one-roll test will specifically tell you if your mattress is too small, too big, or just the right size for your needs.

According to Keenan, there are several other tests you can try to determine if a mattress is right for you. One is called the 30-second “elbow test.” Both sleepers should lie on their backs, place their hands behind their heads, and allow their elbows to point naturally outwards. “If their elbows immediately touch or overlap, it may be a sign they would benefit from more width,” says Keenan. “It takes about 30 seconds, but it gives you a quick visual indication of how much personal space you actually have.”

Both the aforementioned tactics address width, but we can’t forget about height. As a general rule, add at least six inches to the tallest person in bed to ensure their feet do not hang off or press against a footboard. “The easiest way to check is to measure your own height against the length of the mattress, which will show exactly how much clearance is left between your height and the end of the bed,” says Keenan. “If you’re approaching six feet or taller, measure the mattress rather than relying on its label. You ideally want some room beyond your actual height, and the test takes less than a minute.”

Lastly, if you want a simple way to visualize how much space two people will actually share on the mattress, just place two standard pillows side by side on the bed. “The two-pillow test is a really simple way of putting the dimensions into perspective because most people have probably never thought about the width of their pillows in relation to the mattress underneath them,” Keenan explains. “If you already feel like you’re competing for space, seeing that comparison for yourself can make the dimensions much easier to understand. It doesn’t mean everybody sleeping on a double needs to size up, but it does show why the name of a bed size doesn’t necessarily tell you how spacious it will feel when two people are sharing it.”

Before you buy your next mattress, try these simple tests. They’re easy to do and could save you the headache of buying the wrong size for your sleep needs.

#6715. How This Flexible Balm Foundation Held Up to My 12-Hour Day

The skin care-enhanced formula glides on easily and doesn’t quit.

As a mom in my 40s, I ask a lot of my foundation. I have less time to spend in front of the mirror, yet my skin demands more attention: There are acne scars, hyperpigmentation, and redness to cover, plus dryness that makes it tricky to blend makeup seamlessly. Heavy foundations can easily settle into my fine lines, emphasize my pores, and mute my glow. I need a foundation that’s hydrating yet lightweight, with coverage that’s buildable and easy to touch up during long days.

Unfortunately, not many foundations check all of these boxes. I know this firsthand: I’ve been writing about beauty products for more than 15 years and have tested most of the popular options out there. Few seem designed with mature skin in mind, and most tend to become cakey and dry, especially during midday touch-ups.

But L’Oréal Paris True Match Hyaluron Tinted Balm Foundation fits the bill. It’s made using 80 percent skin care ingredients, including skin-plumping hyaluronic acid. That helps the formula hydrate and smooth skin while also minimizing fine lines and wrinkles over time. Here’s my detailed review of how this foundation looked and felt from morning until night.

I tested the Tinted Balm Foundation ahead of a long, meeting-packed weekday. Using the included teardrop-shaped sponge applicator, I applied and blended from the center of my face outward. The narrow tip let me precisely target the corners of my nose and my dark under-eye circles, and build coverage, so I didn’t need concealer. Then, as I blended the foundation across my cheeks and forehead, it melted right in for that elusive second-skin finish.

One thing I noticed right away: This foundation’s luminous tinted-balm texture is unique, in a good way. It sank into my skin for effortless blending and was so lightweight and breathable that I even felt comfortable using it on my chin, which is prone to hormonal acne thanks to perimenopause. Boosting radiance at a time when my skin has been growing more dry and dull, it also blurred imperfections, including the hard-to-cover redness around my nose and melasma on my forehead.

The foundation comes in a neat mirrored compact, which is much more convenient for on-the-go touch-ups than a messy bottle or tube. After applying it, I tucked it into my crossbody pouch before heading out.

I applied my foundation around 7 A.M. to see how it held up during a 12-hour day of working, exercising, carpooling, and socializing. The verdict: This is one foundation that can actually keep up with a woman over 40—and meet her skin needs.

Mid-morning: The first challenge of the day involved walking my dog around the neighborhood. I live on the coast of Maine, where the weather changes by the hour. The wind and salty mist did a number on my blowout, but my foundation still looked freshly applied and flawless when I checked it prior to a 9:30 A.M. video call for work.

Late morning: My foundation was still going strong at 11 A.M., when I hit the gym. Afterward, I slid the compact out for a quick touch-up, mostly reapplying around my nose and covering a couple of chin pimples. I appreciate that the sponge applicator is antimicrobial, which helps prevent bacteria from exacerbating my acne.

Mid-afternoon: Around 3 P.M., after hours at my laptop, I left to cheer my daughters on in back-to-back high school soccer games. This is the time of day when foundation can start to look tired, especially on mature skin. I snuck out the compact for a quick face check. My foundation had not settled into my crow’s feet, and my skin still looked and felt hydrated and comfortable, not parched. A quick touch-up of my dark circles was all it took to keep my makeup looking on point.

Evening: I arrived home around 8:30 P.M., ate a quick dinner with my family, then did one final complexion inspection before showering. After a long day, including many hours out in the elements and two quick foundation touch-ups, my skin still looked fresh and even.

I’d recommend L’Oréal Paris True Match Hylauron Tinted Balm Foundation to any friends over 40 looking for an easygoing foundation that hydrates, minimizes imperfections, and boosts glow. The skin care-enhanced formula and convenient compact make this a top pick for anyone juggling the busy schedules and skin concerns that come with middle age.

For all-day comfort, what should you look for in a foundation for mature skin?

To nurture mature skin, look for a foundation that incorporates skin care ingredients such as hyaluronic acid, which helps hydrate and plump skin and ensures smooth application. The formula should be lightweight and buildable enough to cover imperfections without feeling heavy or settling into fine lines.

How can you reapply foundation without it looking cakey?

Start by choosing a lightweight foundation that’s hydrating and buildable, such as L’Oréal Paris True Match Hyaluron Tinted Balm Foundation, and apply it in thin layers. Then, for touch-ups, use the teardrop sponge applicator to precisely dab and blend makeup just where needed, like around the nose or directly onto blemishes and hyperpigmentation.

#6714. This Historic Napa Valley Estate Features 18th-Century Charm, Fig Trees, and a Luxe Spa

Preserving the past while readying the property for a new chapter was the goal.

At Vine & Fig, each moment takes on a bit of everyday grandeur, spurred on by the sun-drenched warmth and crisp air of Napa County. The aptly named estate, set among vineyards, olive groves, and fig trees in St. Helena, California, has always had the makings of a particularly good gathering place; it just needed a little help catching up with the times.

Enter designer Jaime Rummerfield, who spent four years transforming the 19th-century property into a 10,500-square-foot retreat that feels both comfortable and reverential to its past. The undertaking was hardly a straightforward process. Between fires, floods, and the delicate logistics of preserving the estate’s historic architecture and geothermal streams, Rummerfield had to tackle the renovation with equal parts imagination and restraint. “You cannot approach a place like this as a blank canvas,” she explains. “Understanding what is already there becomes part of the design narrative.”

Fortunately, she had a willing creative partner in homeowner Olivia Marciano, whose background as artistic director of the Marciano Art Foundation brought an especially discerning perspective to the process. The two spent hours considering materials, palettes, and custom details, often allowing a single piece to inform the direction of an entire room. The resulting interiors are richly layered yet approachably livable: antique-style cabinetry with exposed hinges, hand-crafted furnishings, expressive stone, and artwork that lends the rooms a sense of collected history. “Everything shouldn’t feel as though it arrived on the same truck,” Rummerfield says.

Given that this is Napa Valley, it goes without saying that the perks of the property extend well beyond its walls. A chef’s kitchen opens onto the surrounding gardens, while the historic pool house was reimagined as an immersive hammam, complete with Moroccan mosaics, Taj Mahal stone, and copper hot and cold plunge tubs. But for Rummerfield, the property’s greatest luxury isn’t any single amenity, but the new chapter unfolding around it. “There are vineyards, olive groves, gardens, food being prepared, and people coming together,” she says. "The interiors are the stage and participate in all of that.” And at Vine & Fig, there's always room for one more act.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Jaime RummerfieldBuilder: JMA Uncommon BuildersArchitect: 4C Design GroupLandscape Architect: Surfacedesign, Inc Location: St. Helena, CaliforniaThe Space: A six-bedroom, ten-bathroom home across 10,500 square feet

Designed to accommodate everything from quiet family dinners to spirited parties, the kitchen embraces the property’s farm-to-table ethos. Antique-style cabinetry with exposed butt hinges lends the space a sense of history, while a white oak island, stone backsplash, and French trestle table keep things grounded. Beyond the windows, views of the mountains and gardens reinforce the connection between the home and its surroundings.

“We wanted the practical decisions and the decorative decisions to support one another,” adds Rummerfield. “The cabinetry details, hidden technology, ample storage, textiles, and connection to the outdoors keep it feeling like a room in a home. It is a chef’s delight, but it is also a very welcoming heart of the house.”

“We wanted this multi-use kitchen to have a personality appropriate to its setting while still feeling connected to the wider property, hammam, and pool,” says Rummerfield of this bonus cook space. “The proportions and material relationships become especially important. It ended up being one of our favorite spaces—the rich chocolate zellige tile back splash really made it unique and divine.”

Few amenities feel more appropriate to a Napa Valley estate than a dedicated wine cave. Here, Rummerfield let the architecture and natural materials establish the atmosphere, incorporating honed black soapstone countertops to create a space that feels equally suited to storing prized bottles and sharing a favorite vintage with friends.

Rummerfield bypassed a rigid furniture arrangement in favor of several intimate seating areas, creating a room that feels equally suited to lively conversation and afternoon lounging. “I wanted the living room to have a sense of occasion while remaining completely comfortable,” she says.

To that end, a generously proportioned, low-slung sectional offers a particularly inviting place to land, while custom cabinetry conceals modern technology, including a drop-down projector screen, without interrupting the room’s collected aesthetic.

“Passageways are wonderful places to create a moment of discovery,” Rummerfield says of the dramatic entryway and curved staircase, which features the client’s art colleciton. “I want these spaces to feel as personal, stylish and welcoming as the rooms they connect to.”

An arched ceiling and soft lighting sets the tone for the journey into the property’s reimagined pool house, where Rummerfield wanted to create “a completely different experience” within the property. “The hammam is about slowing down and enjoying the beauty of the materials,” she adds. “The barrel ceiling, Moroccan mosaic, and Taj Mahal stone give the space a wonderful sense of intimacy and ritual.”

“Powder rooms offer a lovely opportunity for personalization; the relationship between the vanity, sink, mirror, and light can give the room a distinct personality,” says Rummerfield. In here, she sources a Turkish sink from Olive Ateliers, incorporated a 1940’s glass sconce, inlaid an antique mirror, and brought the terra cotta paver floors from outside in. “Each element is deliberately chosen and work beautifully together,” she adds.

Affectionately dubbed the “Strawberry Room” after the pattern on the BDDW chair, this bedroom offers comfort and relaxation in space. Rummerfield leaned into the room’s cozy proportions with a tactile Phillip Jeffries wallcovering, layered fabrics, and sculptural lighting by Ken Fulk for Urban Electric.

“I like a bathroom to feel considered as a room in its own right,” says Rummerfield. “The vanity, mirror, lighting, and tub should have a pleasing relationship, with the materials doing much of the decorative work. The atmosphere should feel quiet and indulgent.”

“I enjoy giving utility spaces some personality,” Rummerfield says of the laundry room, which she coated in Benjamin Moore’s Spring Valley, a fresh green that nods to the surrounding landscape. “Many people spend time in them, so the light, finishes, and everyday experience deserve attention.”

Jaime Rummerfield is a Los Angeles–based interior designer known for her richly layered spaces that balance timeless elegance with a distinct sense of personality. Her work celebrates craftsmanship, architectural character, and the art of collecting, combining vintage finds, custom furnishings, and expressive materials to create interiors that feel both sophisticated and lived-in.
