#6712. Rosalía Wins With Her Style at the 2026 Latin Grammy Awards

She wore a quartet of designer looks from Matières Fécales, Erdem, Vivienne Westwood, and Dior

Rosalía won big at the 27th annual Latin Grammy Awards last night. For her fourth studio album, Lux, the Spanish pop singer took home her third trophy for Album of the Year, becoming the first woman in the history of the ceremony to ever do so. She collected a total of five awards, coming out on top in other major categories like Song of the Year, which was awarded to her track “La Perla” (featuring Yahritza y Su Esencia), and Best Short Form Music Video, which went to her visual for “Sauvignon Blanc.” And to coincide with such a major night, Rosalía brought out a few major designer looks, all styled by Chloe & Chenelle Delgadillo.

To start the evening, the “Berghain” singer hit the red carpet in the avant-garde label of the moment, Matières Fécales, which has been embraced by everyone from Zendaya to Rihanna and Demi Moore.

Courtesy of the label’s Spring/Summer 2027 collection, Rosalía’s angelic white gown featured a ruched tulle bodice with semi-sheer folds that hugged her body and culminated in the house’s signature déchiré detailing. The tattered fragments arched from Rosalía’s shoulders like wings, while also dangling from the ends of her cropped sleeves. Down low, there was equal drama courtesy of a dramatic tulle bubble skirt that trailed around her in a voluminous cascade. Of course, her signature halo-dyed hair acted as the perfect complement to the heavenly ensemble.

Later, Rosalía hit the stage to perform a medley of songs from Lux, including “La Perla,” “La Rumba del Perdón,” and “Dios Es un Stalker.” For her performance, she wore a bespoke satin gown from Erdem, pulled from the house’s Fall/Winter 2026 collection. (Just last month, she turned to the same London-based label for a stunning balletcore street-style moment.)

The golden-hued design featured a corset-style bodice and knotted bows along the neckline, with a basque waist and flared ruffle skirt that was overlayed with black tulle and trimmed with ostrich feathers.

Once she returned to her seat, it turned out Rosalía had another surprise look in store, as she changed into a custom white dress from Vivienne Westwood. The bridal-inspired design featured a strapless, plunging sweetheart bodice decorated with a tiny bow in the middle, while layers of fabric were draped along the waist for a subtle peplum effect.

All of the fabric, meanwhile, was embroidered with floral lace details and microscopic pearl embellishments, and the singer wore a scarf to match, while slipping into a pair of contrasting black pumps.

To finish her quartet of looks, Rosalía switched into a custom black Dior dress by Jonathan Anderson, who, fittingly, also helped design many of the looks for the Spanish superstar’s recent Lux Tour.

The silky black slip featured two delicate lace straps that hung off Rosalía’s shoulders, while the bust tied into a knot that dangled in front like a tassel. The top half of the dress was decorated with subtle floral details, while the skirt featured panels of semi-sheer material. It all worked perfectly to serve as an inky backdrop as Rosalía posed with her stack of gramophone trophies.

As she accepted her prize for Album of the Year, Rosalía got teary-eyed, saying: “This is the third time I’ve won this award. If El Mal Querer was a dream, and Motomami was a bold gamble, then Lux has been a leap into the unknown. I believe it’s very important to speak your own truth—and for others to be able to speak theirs—and this record is mine. I thank God, and I thank my loved ones: my family and my friends. Thank you for your love and for shaping me into who I am today. The truth is, I know I still have a lot to learn, and I’m certain that I likely haven’t even created my best work yet.”

#6711. Time to Revisit Julianne Hough’s Dating History for Some Seriously Hunky Reasons

The concept of her attending the Met Gala with Ryan Seacrest… twice.

Julianne Hough has graced our screens for nearly two decades thanks to her ties to Dancing With the Stars and projects like Safe Haven and Burlesque. For someone who’s been in the entertainment biz for a minute, Julianne has surprisingly kept her personal life under wraps. Well, for the most part.

Aside from her music, dancing skills, and red carpet looks, the media personality has also made headlines for her rumored romances and high-profile relationships. (Because who could forget she dated Ryan Seacrest back in the day? Certainly not this writer who lived through it.)

Anyway! As she interviews celebs in the DWTS sky box and joins them for numbers on the dance floor, you might be wondering who she’s romantically linked to these days. Ahead, find Julianne Hough’s full dating history, including some surprise rumors.

Before Julianne moved out to L.A. to be a pro on DWTS’s early seasons, she was romantically linked to her childhood sweetheart, Zack Wilson, who she had known since she was nine.

“I was engaged to someone else, a guy I knew from Utah who had served a Mormon mission,” she said during a 2012 interview with Glamour, adding that he was “a great guy.”

They ended up calling off their engagement in November 2007, and according to Julianne, it fizzled out because she was ~soul-searching.~

“I needed to figure myself out more, and so I moved to L.A. and we ended it,” she shared, adding that he went on to marry and have a kid with her childhood best friend.

“It was a little weird when it happened, but they’re a great couple!” she admitted.

Okay, so her engagement didn’t work out—but it led to her first high-profile relationship with country singer Chuck Wicks after they met on Brad Paisley’s tour in August 2008.

It was truly a whirlwind romance, since he publicly declared they were “very much in love” on red carpets and even gave her a birthday shoutout on stage. By December that year, they had apparently discussed potentially getting married.

Of course, the pair walked many a red carpet and even became partners on DWTS in 2009.

They had reportedly had a “mutual” split by November 2009, and though things seemed amicable at the time, Chuck admitted that “shit hit the fan” nearly a decade later.

“I’m not going to throw anybody under the bus but … it wasn’t my fault,” he said on his Talk to Chuck podcast in 2020, adding that he “played the game” and kept things civil in public, though he “didn’t really like her at the time.”

“Now, I just don't even care,” he admitted. “It’s on you. You get past it. I’ve seen her one time. I’ve seen her one time in 10 years.”

There’s not much to go on here, since the Safe Haven actor and comedian never confirmed whether they were an item. However, in March 2010, a source told People that Dane had “kissed her, and they quickly left together” after he headlined a comedy show.

They were seen together in West Hollywood one more time before moving on.

Though Julianne claimed a friend tried to set her up with the American Idol host when she was still engaged to Zack, she didn’t give him a chance until things ended with Chuck years later. During the aforementioned Glamour interview, she revealed she had properly met Ryan when she and Chuck appeared on his radio show as a couple.

“I blush when I hear [the interview] because Ryan is hitting on me the entire time!” she shared. “At the end, Ryan said something like, ‘You guys really have something special!’ And I thought to myself, ‘He’s so full of shit right now!’ When Chuck and I broke up, I wanted to be single. That lasted five months. Then Ryan and I went on a date.”

Her interview with Chuck was seemingly scrubbed from the internet (womp), but she appeared on Ryan’s radio show many times after. This was pretty much the vibe every time:

Anyway, their one date eventually turned into a 3-year relationship, complete with Golden Globes, Emmys, and two Met Gala appearances.

According to People’s sources, they eventually split in March 2013 because of “busy work schedules,” though they’re still cool to this day.

When asked whether it was awkward for the exes to chat on-record during a joint interview with her brother, Derek Hough, in 2022, Ryan said, “There is nothing uncomfortable about it because we remained friends for years and still are.”

Julianne maintained that they still had “respect and love” for each other after their split.

After her split with Ryan, Julianne was reportedly linked to Oscar-winning filmmaker Ari Sandel in July 2013 when they were spotted making out at Nobu.

In August 2013, they were photographed leaving a party at the Chateau Marmont and grabbed brunch in West Hollywood the next day. They never publicly commented on their relationship, so it’s unclear how long they actually dated, if at all.

This is Julianne’s biggest and most public relationship to date. She met former hockey star Brooks Laich through mutual friends before they started a long-distance relationship in 2013. (He lived in Washington, D.C. while Julianne was still based in L.A.)

They eventually got engaged in 2015 and tied the knot at an outdoor ceremony on Idaho’s Lake Coeur d’Alene in 2017. However, their romance would come to an end by May 2020, when they announced their split in a joint statement to People.

“We have lovingly and carefully taken the time we have needed to arrive at our decision to separate. We share an abundance of love and respect for one another and will continue to lead with our hearts from that place,” they said at the time.

During an August 2024 appearance on Dax Shepard’s Armchair Expert podcast, Julianne admitted that she and Brooks were “sad that it didn’t work out” between them—especially since he’s remained close with her brother, Derek.

“I feel like our relationship and our marriage was exactly right, and the end of it is right, too,” she explained at the time. “We both have regrets that it didn’t work out because I don’t think we had the maturity to come together. He was contracting while I was expanding. We just couldn’t find each other in that.”

Back in December 2024, Julianne was photographed after a dinner date with Bachelor Nation alum Tanner Courtad. That was the only time we had seen anything about the pair…until Tanner fully spilled the tea during the Bachelor Happy Hour podcast in November 2026.

“That was short-lived, but it was fun. I was going to Dancing With the Stars to support Joey [Graziadei], and I was sitting in the front row, and Ezra [Sosa] came over to me, and he goes, ‘Hey, our host thinks you’re cute. Can I give you her number?’” he shared.

He continued, “I actually met her out in the parking lot. We talked for a couple of minutes, decided to go on a date and talked for a little bit. We went to the park with her dog, got some sushi, hung out for the night and that’s when we got spotted by the paparazzi is picking up sushi.”

Though they never kept seeing each other, Tanner confirmed, “It was very short-lived. Julianne’s great, but it was never anything more than just, like, a singular date.”

That’s all for now, but we’re actively rooting for Julianne and her hot girl agenda.

#6710. The Launch: November’s Must-Know Fashion News

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Fall is in full swing, and with it, so is the fashion industry. As the leaves continue to turn and preparations for the holiday season get serious, brands are finding new ways to celebrate with exciting new capsules, collaborations, and stocking-worthy releases. Look no further than J.J. Martin, whose new book, Table Energy, explores the art of Italian entertaining, and Rimowa x Maison Margiela, which turns travel into something even more desirable. Below, all the fashion news to know this month.

What: High jewelry collection and new boutique

Where: 237 Worth Ave, Palm Beach, Florida

Why: A 1920s Palm Beach estate is a fitting setting for Bulgari’s first U.S. presentation of “Eclettica.” Designed by Addison Mizner, the waterfront home hosted a gala that also marked the opening of the house’s new boutique. Venus Williams, Chanel Iman, and Becca Bloom were among the guests, with Jewel performing. The collection includes 15 transformable designs—the most Bulgari has presented in a single collection—alongside more than 50 high jewelry pieces. With its Roman roots and sculptural approach to high jewelry, Bulgari’s “Eclettica” brings a distinctly Italian point of view to Palm Beach.

Where: Online at rimowa.com and maisonmargiela.com and select Rimowa and Maison Margiela stores

Why: Some things look better after they’ve been lived in. That idea is at the heart of Rimowa and Maison Margiela’s new travel collaboration, which brings together two signatures: the German luggage house’s familiar grooves and Margiela’s hand-painted white Bianchetto finish. Designed to change with use, the suitcase and matching bag will take on marks and patina as they travel. A campaign by Dexter Lander brings that idea to life in a Haussmannian apartment, where the white paint seems to spill beyond the pieces themselves.

What: Bird on a Rock installation, window displays, and holiday décor

Where: The Landmark; 727 5th Ave, New York City

Why: Tiffany & Co. has switched on the holiday season at The Landmark, with Amanda Seyfried leading the way. The actress lit the flagship’s Bird on a Rock installation by placing an ornament on its Blue Box tree, marking the third year of the display and a full-circle moment. Seyfried stars in the house’s new holiday film, “You & Me,” which travels between present-day New York and a memory from the ’90s. Mikey Madison, Jordyn Woods, and Louisa Jacobson joined the celebration.

Tiffany & Co. Bird on a Rock Wings Narrow Ring

Why: Just in time for the holidays, La DoubleJ founder J.J. Martin has released Table Energy, a new book about the art of Italian entertaining, with a foreword by fashion writer Leandra Medine Cohen. Its pages move from alfresco lunches to candlelit dinners, all in the colorful, convivial spirit of La DoubleJ. The book made its debut at the brand’s Upper East Side boutique, The Lighthouse, where Martin, ELLE Decor’s head of editorial content Elisa Lipsky-Karasz, and ELLE editor-in-chief Nina Garcia gathered to celebrate l’art de la table.

Where: Online at tecovas.com, aninebing.com, and select retail locations

Why: Anine Bing has a new way to kick up a look. The designer has teamed up with Tecovas on a capsule that reworks the label’s Loretta and Jamie boots with star embroidery and polished goat leather. Cowboy hats and belts round out the lineup, which launches alongside Anine Bing’s spring/summer 2027 collection.

#6709. Jennifer Lawrence Swaps Her Pops of Lemon for a Polished Dark Monochrome Look

Complete with a glossy Hermès crocodile Kelly

Jennifer Lawrence can just as easily pull off a bright lemon-yellow Colleen Allen coat or a pair of funky sequined pink Siedrés pants as she can a polished, monochromatic business look.

Earlier this week, the actor looked so chic in an outfit composed of timeless navy and black staples. The base of her look was made up of an elongated, buttoned blazer, and matching trousers. To continue the color scheme, she wrapped a navy scarf around her neck.

Then came the luxurious black accents: square sunglasses and a rare Hermès Kelly Sellier in shiny crocodile leather and gold hardware. And because Lawrence cannot live without her pops of color, the actor slipped on a pair of burgundy velvet slippers.

Lawrence is often one to go minimalist with her jewelry and glam, and she did that here, opting for polish-free nails, minimal makeup, and a few gold rings (one of them being her engagement ring from husband Cooke Maroney).

As you may have guessed, the cherry on top of the look was Lawrence’s dirty-blonde hair, which was styled down in easy, breezy long waves. The Hunger Games alum recently introduced her haircare brand, Mes, and has since been promoting her products around town.

“I feel like if my hair looks good, no one will notice anything else about my face, my clothes, or my body,” the actor joked in a recent interview with The Cut.

Of course, we disagree. While we do love a good hair day, we’ll never miss a J.Law street-style moment.

#6708. This Designer-Approved Curtain Trick Will Instantly Elevate Your Bedroom

Your window treatments should serve up both form and function.

Window treatments are often an afterthought, but your choice of drapery can make or break your entire design. Our 2026 Whole Home in Charleston is a master class in innovative design, and our designers took every opportunity to showcase their skills and style, down to the very last detail—including some gorgeous examples of transformative drapery.

Though often seen as a luxury, window treatments are a home necessity. Blinds, drapes, and shades don’t just line our windowpanes; they block drafts, light, and real-world clamor from entering your homes, practically on demand. While some choose to keep it simple, designers know your sill is uncharted territory for design potential. While it might be tempting to reach for your typical verticals, it’s worth making a statement. That said, good drapes can be expensive, so if you’re hoping to embrace your window treatment’s potential, you’ll have to budget for it.

In our patternmaxxed guest bedroom, designer Laura Lubin, founder of the Nashville-based design firm Ellerslie Interiors, used Stroheim drapes to add subtle color to her muted palette and elevate the chic TWOPAGES Roman shades just beyond. Then she took her design a step further: Instead of relying on pleating for texture, Lubin added a leafy trim and Fabricut bullion fringe to the edge of her panels, framing the bold blue baseboard and adding another note of texture to the room’s design.

Texture is meant to act as a bridge in design schemes, connecting elements and feeding both our visual and tactile appetites. In addition to her customized drapes, Lubin used grid-patterned wallpaper, natural wood grain, and plaid textiles to elevate the visual palette.

If you’re looking for a quick bedroom upgrade with big impact, it might be time to take a closer look at your drapes. After all, your window treatments are meant to block out the world, not your style.

#6704. I Found the Most Comfortable Pointed-Toe Boots

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Buying pointed-toe boots has always been a challenge for me because I’m built like a ballerina—not, sadly, in terms of long legs or elegant arms or a swanlike neck, but in terms of feet. I have the bunions of a woman who has dedicated her life to the art of dance, which is ironic because I took ballet lessons for all of three months when I was eight. (I quit after they let me wear the tutu, which was really the only thing I was in it for.) I am confessing this not because I enjoy talking about my feet on the internet, but to establish my credibility when I say that I have finally found a pair of slim, sexy, pointed-toe boots that are comfortable enough to wear all day, even for those of us with less-than-ideal extremities.

Margaux’s Inga boots have a long, squared-off toe and a little kitten heel that measures 1.5 inches—tall enough to give you a bit of a lift, but low enough to be walkable. They come in black leather and a sleek espresso suede, both of which look great with jeans but would also be cute peeking out of long, drapey trousers. The best part is the sizing: Margaux offers its boots in narrow, medium, and wide, and somehow, through the magic of proportion play, the wide does not look wide. They accommodate the fullest part of my foot without looking bulky or breaking up the nice long line.

The first time I wore them, it was to a witches’ dinner that my friend Mo throws every Halloween. She invites 30 of her best female friends over, decorates her home in high goth style, and serves us a theatrically witchy meal. (This time, it was black squid-ink pasta.) Of course, one has to dress appropriately to dine with the coven, so I paired the boots with slim black jeans and a sheer black polka-dotted top. Not only did I get a bunch of compliments, but I didn’t find myself pining for my broomstick on the walk home—the boots were just as comfortable at the end of the evening as they’d been at the start of the night.

They’ve held up equally well on the commute to work. In addition to the extra width, they also come with five millimeters of cushioning on the inside, which is enough to protect you when you’re pounding the pavement. Ever since slimmer toes reentered the fashion conversation, I’ve been faced with the choice between sitting out the trend or hobbling around in boots that fundamentally do not fit. As much as I love a good pair of shoes, I also live for a long walk, and the Ingas, miraculously, let me have both. Really, they don’t feel like heels at all, which is a rare and wonderful thing for a pair of pointed-toe kitten-heel boots.

#6703. 8 Popular Home Items From 20 Years Ago That Are Making a Surprising Comeback

They may be a blast from the past, but they’re about to be very now.

Trends come and go, but they almost always come back again. If there’s one thing we know about interior design, it’s that what feels old and outdated manages to become fresh and cool again in a few years’ time. Skeptics can easily be proved wrong with the resurgence of sunken living rooms, glass block walls, and even cushy sofas plucked right out of a Nora Ephron movie. And, as younger generations continue to be intrigued with the 1990s and early 2000s, they are turning to the era’s interiors for inspiration.

Well, sort of, as Maggie Vollrath of Provision Studios points out. “When we look back at certain interiors from the 2000s, there was such a commitment to achieving a particular look that every element in the room was chosen to reinforce it,” the Georgia designer says. “A home should feel like a collection of things you genuinely love, not as though you’ve walked onto a movie set designed to represent a particular style.”

When you cherry-pick trends and design details from the past, you’re able to create a home that honors the past without appearing stuck in the past. “The goal isn’t to make a home look like it belongs to another decade,” Vollrath says. “You want to bring back the beauty and character of those decorative details while creating something that feels authentic to both the house and its owners.”

Below, designers share trends from the past that the next generation of design lovers adore.

For older generations, a swivel chair might conjure memories of sitting in your family’s den, but designers promise these movin’, groovin’ seats have been revived for the 21st century. “Swivel barrel chairs defined early-2000s transitional interiors, usually in a bright flat polyester-blend upholstery,” explains Hallie Malitsky, principal designer of Hallie Rose Design in Brooklyn. “The version coming back today is a different experience entirely.”

Today’s iterations, she insists, are subtler. Cheap polyester has yielded to rich, tactile fabrics like wool, linen, and velvet. For a timely twist, she also encourages trading the fire engine red and butter yellow popular in the 2000s for burgundy and ochre. “Surround it with natural materials like wood, linen, and stone, and [your chair] will stop being the loudest thing in the room,” she recommends.

When Malitsky thinks of early aughts design, she immediately envisions the gigantic walnut media center that plagued many of her friends’ family rooms. “Dark wood cabinetry is a notorious culprit of bad 2000s design, but the wood wasn’t the issue,” she says. “It was the fact that there was so much of it.”

Now that dark wood tones are making a comeback, Malitsky says to use them sparingly. Two to three walnut or ebony pieces per room will make enough of a statement. And, no: Unlike the living rooms of yore, your furniture doesn’t have to match.

“What I love about their return is that we’re incorporating these pieces in a much more collected way,” shares Vollrath. “Rather than purchasing an entire matching furniture collection, we’re mixing periods and bringing in individual pieces that have character and history.”

The early aughts had lots of elaborate cabinetry, replete with corbels, substantial cornices, and beautifully detailed range hoods. As everyone—yes, even younger generations—continue to favor more traditional interiors, we’re seeing a renewed interest in ornamented cabinetry.

“There’s something really special about a kitchen that feels as though it could have been there for generations, or even reminds you of your grandmother’s kitchen,” Vollrath says. “Those decorative flourishes bring a sense of history, craftsmanship, and permanence that can be difficult to achieve with a more streamlined approach.”

But why stop there when you can add character to other rooms of the house?

“We’re seeing a broader interest in architectural detailing throughout the home, from wainscoting and wall paneling to ceiling beams and substantial moldings,” she adds. “Homeowners are becoming more interested in understanding these elements, considering them during renovations, and making them a priority in new construction.”

If the rise of analog interiors proves anything, it’s that even the best chatbot can’t beat excellent design. Instead of having a home covered in screens and smart systems, many are favoring more primitive setups that harken back to dial-up and CD-ROMs. For example, Fargo-based designer Lindsey Christie has been noticing (and loving) the return of landlines. “There's something wonderfully nostalgic about a beautiful telephone sitting on a bedside table or tucked into a thoughtfully designed corner of a home,” she says.

Of course, there’s plenty of fun to be had in a “dumb home.” “We have had several adult clients wanting to incorporate their hobby of Legos into their design aesthetic,” reveals Allison Smith of Gem + Clay Interiors in St. Augustine, Florida. “I definitely think this speaks to a desire to return to the analog, and that [old-school] element.”

“We always joke about the Tuscan kitchens from 2005, but they had some good things going on,” says Drew Michael Scott of Lone Fox. If you strip away the “bottles of preserved peppers and gaudy oil-rubbed bronze faucets,” the style was actually rocking a great color palette. Shades like brown, ochre, terracotta, and deep red bring a little bit of the great outdoors inside, plus they can pair nicely with today’s cozy interiors. But let’s keep the overly Italian influences in the past, shall we?

Today’s emerging design lovers are extra—just ask Scott, who has been noticing a considerable uptick in passementerie. “These intricate details were everywhere in the mid-2000s, before the world decided everything should be sleek and gray,” he says. “They’re becoming popular again, and I think they really evoke the attention to detail we saw at the turn of the century.”

Though yesteryear’s tassels, beading, and decorative trim are undeniably fun, using them requires a little bit of strategy. Since there’s a fine line between charming and kitschy, balance these louder, upholstered moments with pared-back wooden pieces.

Goodbye, panel-ready refrigerators and dishwashers: Katie Kiser says stainless steel appliances are so back. According to the Tennessee-based designer, the resurgence is a break from the picture-perfect kitchens older generations have pined over. “Gen-Z values practicality over perfection,” she explains. “I think it will show up in their interiors.” And why stop at appliances when you can bring that lived-in feel to the rest of the space? “They don’t hate clutter the way millennials do,” she adds. “We’ll see details like cookbooks in the kitchens and hall trees with tennis rackets on them.”

Though Scott hasn’t seen the Venetian Gold granite that ruled Y2K just yet, he’s noticing many homeowners are willing to get creative with their countertops.

“We've moved beyond using only Carrara marble in kitchens,” he explains.

Ditto for quartz, which some designers believe is on the outs. Instead, Scott says travertine is huge again and granite is poised to make the comeback. If you want to keep these materials from appearing dated, he encourages you to reach for stones with bolding veining and inclusions.

#6702. This London Townhouse Was Designed for the Holidays

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Miles Redd and David Kaihoi created the ultimate English Christmas retreat for longtime clients—cozy, festive, and swathed in reds and greens.

Above: In the living room, the seating is by Luther Quintana with Samuel & Sons fringe. The wallcovering is by Iksel, and the cocktail table has a Fornasetti top.

Some homes are for summers by the water, others for weekends on the slopes. When Krystyna Houser set about redecorating her family’s London townhouse, she was thinking of Christmas. “We spend every Christmas there,” she says. At this house, that meant more than just putting up a tree.

Located near Richmond Park, in southwestern London, the 19th-century townhouse has been in Houser’s family for more than 20 years. It was always a place to return to while Houser, founder of the nutritional startup mBIOTA Elemental, and her husband Dan, a co-founder of Rockstar Games, were raising their three children in New York City. Dan had grown up nearby, and the ample greenspace that gives the area its name made it an appealing place to visit with a young family. The interiors, however, had never quite received their due.

Houser turned to Miles Redd and David Kaihoi, the partners behind the New York–based firm Redd Kaihoi. She had known Redd since 2012, when she hired him to decorate a Brooklyn Heights townhouse, and the two became friends. They have since worked on multiple houses together. For London she trusted the designers to craft the warm and celebratory atmosphere she craved. “I wanted it to be equally elegant, cozy, and family-oriented,” she says.

Red and green were a natural starting point, as green already had the family’s allegiance. “It’s one of their favorite colors,” Redd says. “So we leaned into that a lot.” Verdant hues appear in velvet, felt, and marble throughout, while the entrance hall is dressed in crimson. These were holiday colors with no intention of coming down in January, and Redd was going for sophistication rather than holiday kitsch. “I wanted romantic 19th-century elegance, the feeling that they’re stepping into a Merchant Ivory movie.”

This approach suits a firm known for exuberant color and layered furnishings. Redd brings a sense of theater to traditional decorating, drawing on the work of such legendary designers as Billy Baldwin, Albert Hadley, and John Fowler and mixing lacquer, chintz, and bold geometric patterns. Before opening his practice in 1998, he trained with antiques dealer John Rosselli and decorator Bunny Williams. Kaihoi, a sculptor, joined the ELLE Decor A-List firm in 2008 and became a partner in 2019. Together the duo give grand rooms an ease and wit that keep formality in check.

Houser considers “interior designer” an insufficient description for Redd and Kaihoi. “I feel like they are set curators,” she says. She gave them considerable freedom, occasionally inviting them to surprise her with potential purchases. “I don’t think it’s my place to hinder creative expression, and, as a result, the rooms are just magical.”

The house’s existing architecture gave that freedom some useful structure. “It had great English bones,” Redd says. He retained the original living room mantelpiece, library doors, and front door, while upstairs he and Kaihoi worked to restore character lost to earlier alterations that had stripped away 19th-century details. An excavation created additional basement space for a screening room and a ping-pong area, giving the family more places to gather and play.

From first visit to completion, the project took the better part of an unhurried decade, with pauses for permits, the Covid pandemic, and other commitments along the way. All the while Redd and Kaihoi kept shopping at auctions, antiques stores, and fairs, which ensured that the rooms would have an accumulated character. The designers looked to the guards at Buckingham Palace for the entrance hall and main staircase, borrowing the red and gold of their uniforms for its matka silk walls trimmed with gold braiding. Beyond the hall, the green velvet–lined library opens into a living room papered to resemble a coromandel screen.

Upstairs, the primary bedroom and a sitting area occupy a long, narrow space. Green felt screen dividers give the bed its own area in one corner without a wall breaking up the suite. “It’s all sort of one big bedsit, which is a very English notion,” Redd says. A small painting of a parrot on one of the screens keeps Houser company at her bedside.

The couple wanted their suite to feel like a luxurious hotel—inviting places to read and work, a comfortable bed, and a generously scaled bathroom—without sacrificing a sense of home. “You’ve ruined hotels for us,” Redd recalls Houser telling him of their previous work.

Step Inside the Ultimate Christmas Retreat

But Houser’s favorite rooms are the ones that bring everyone together: the kitchen and the adjoining striped family room, which open onto the garden. It’s here where everyone gathers for meals and to relax. The connected spaces provide separate places to eat, play games, and watch television, allowing the family to spend time together even when everyone is doing something different.

“Their design helps bring us together,” Houser says. “And that’s what it is. It’s a family home.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

#6700. Drew Barrymore Winks to Her 2000s Style in a Black Newsboy Cap

Anyone else thinking of that scene from Charlie’s Angels: Full Throttle?

Last night, the Museum of Modern Art held its annual film benefit in tandem with Chanel, which raises money for “the acquisition and preservation of key film works” in the MoMA’s movie department, with director Steven Spielberg serving as this year’s special honoree. Typically, the New York City event unites some of the starriest names in Hollywood, and the 2026 benefit was no exception, with attendees including the likes of Cate Blanchett, Zoe Saldaña, Emily Blunt, and Michelle Pfeiffer. But even at such an A-list event, one person managed to steal the show: Drew Barrymore.

The talk show host turned heads in a striking, Mod-inspired look, the star of which was a shaggy black-and-white jacket. Meanwhile, her choice of headwear injected the evening with a welcome dose of nostalgia.

Like most of the attendees, Barrymore dressed in Chanel for the occasion, pulling a statement piece of outerwear from the house’s Pre-Fall 2026 collection by Matthieu Blazy. The jacket featured a black-and-white windowpane pattern in a distorted tweed, with fringe tufts running all along the trim. Meanwhile, the lining featured a pattern of repeating faces in red and white, which only peeked out in brief moments.

Stylist Lee Harris left the jacket open to reveal a black turtleneck top and matching slacks underneath, while a long pearl necklace adorned with the house’s signature double-C pendant dangled in front.

However, it was Barrymore’s black newsboy cap that made the greatest statement. Over the years, the Emmy Award-winner has been a champion of all kinds of hat styles, from fedoras and beanies to baseball caps and berets. But you might recall that she went through a strong newsboy-cap phase in the early 2000s, especially when she was promoting the Charlie’s Angels films.

In fact, she event wears one during one of the most-memorable scenes in the sequel, Charlie’s Angels: Full Throttle (2003), when she and co-stars Cameron Diaz and Lucy Liu have an impromptu dance break to MC Hammer’s “U Can’t Touch This.” (You can revisit that incredible moment here.)

In honor of Barrymore, and in honor of cinema, it might just be about time for a rewatch.

#6699. 36 Thoughtful Kitchen Gifts for Anyone Who Loves to Cook, Host, or Sip in Style

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Anyone can buy a candle. These gifts are way more personal.

The best kind of gift shows the person you care about that you know them. That you’ve considered their hobbies. After all, anyone can go out and buy a scented candle, but you are thinking about how they like to spend their free time and what makes them happy. So if you know a dinner party host, mixologist, home chef, or someone who loves a stylish kitchen, then this is the gift guide for you.

Below, dozens of smart and thoughtful kitchen gifts await you. The busy home cook will love an Instant Pot or multipurpose air fryer. The coffee or tea obsessive will love a matcha station or coffee grinder. Think outside the kitchen and into the dining room, too: an elegant yet budget-friendly canister set or berry basket could be just what they need to complete their tablescape.

The best thing about so many of our picks is that there’s a massive price range, so if you see something affordable your friend or family member may like, you don’t need to wait for a special occasion to get it for them. On the other hand, if the same person is celebrating a birthday or a holiday, feel free to splurge. We have everything any at-home cook would love and use.

No kitchen is complete without a good bottle opener, so why not opt for one that’s extra chic? Trade your gas station souvenir opener for this colorful beaded piece, inspired by the classic abacus tool. You can even rearrange the beads to suit your own aesthetic.

Any serious coffee drinker or amateur barista will know the importance of a good coffee bean grinder. There’s nothing quite like being able to control the coarseness of your own coffee for your morning brew. You can choose between 60 grind sizes, whether you’re using a moka pot or prepping cold brew.

If your date night dinner isn’t complete without a round of oysters, then this oyster plate needs to be added to your collection. The ridged porcelain edge looks like a more elegant version of a paper plate. And the indents in the dish are perfect for your East Coast dozen. It’s even dishwasher-safe.

This is the ideal martini glass for your most stylish friend. The color-blocked stems are thin enough for a swirl, and the set of two glasses comes in alternating colors. It even comes with stainless steel swizzle sticks, primed for stuffed olives.

Gift a set of serving utensils that’s a major upgrade from the typical silicone and plastic. The lustrous finish and curved handles are just too beautiful. It’s carved from sustainably sourced horn and natural shell, and it’ll make your salad bowl look like the star of the show.

Rocco’s full-size fridges have gone viral for every sophisticated host. But the latest launch is way more budget-friendly. This mini mini fridge is sized for desktops and nightstands, so whether you need a nighttime face mask or a midday Diet Coke break, it’s stored in style.

Everyone knows at least one matcha drinker who is serious about their habit. This is the station they’ll reach for every morning. The bowl, bamboo whisk, whisk stand, and sifter all ensure a clump-free, perfectly aerated brew. And it’s all gathered on a ceramic, non-slip tea tray.

Know a tinned fish lover? Fishwife has made sardines sexy again, and this advent calendar provides 12 days of fun surprises for every charcuterie board assembler. It even comes with a few non-edible pieces like a fish knife, key chain, and moisturizer.

Associate Shopping Editor Janae McKenzie has been using the electric kettle from Drew Barrymore’s line for years. It comes in a variety of pastel colors that brighten up your countertop and make short work of everything from loose-leaf tea to soup.

Any home chef serious about their spices needs a mortar and pestle that can keep up. It’s made from pure bronze, with an ergonomic handle that makes grinding peppercorns, cardamom, ginger, and garlic easy. It’s handmade by artisans in Moradabad, known as “The Brass City of India.”

These glasses look like they should be kept safe in a china cabinet, but they’re durable enough for everyday life. The shape of the glass was designed in collaboration with WhistlePig distillers to enhance the whiskey-drinking experience. The pontil mark on the bottom of each glass celebrates “perfect imperfection”—a mark leftover by Simon Pearce glassblowers.

A new paper towel holder is an upgrade you won’t think to do on your own, which makes it the perfect kitchen gift. It’s likely one of the most-visited spots in your kitchen. The painted beech wood and rubber feet keep it both stylish and secure.

Things get messy in the kitchen, and no one will say no to a gift of fresh dish towels. These are woven from organic cotton and stitched by a woman-owned textile company in Mumbai. They’re embroidered with the icons of Indian cooking: a fresh coconut, a tomato, and curry leaves.

Ash wood and granite stone are a match made in heaven to get the most of your ground pepper. McKenzie uses this with gourmet peppercorns, and with the easily refilled vessel and cut stone, it’s a tactile experience that elevates the simplest meal. The stone smooths over time as oils are released by the peppercorns, effectively seasoning it over time.

This is perfect for the pitmaster in your life. Sure, they've probably got a meat thermometer from the grocery store, but this upgrade is so worth it, especially for those long, slow-cooked meats. This high-tech, long-range Bluetooth thermometer will save them the headache of constantly checking their grill, so they can check its status from the comfort of the couch.

This is the potluck host’s secret weapon. No need to mess with the hassle of foil pans, chafing dishes, and Sterno burners. Just one glass-top electric warming tray can keep your appetizers hot for the whole party. It even comes with cool-touch handles for easy portability.

This is the hand wash McKenzie leaves out on her kitchen sink when guests come over. “The Moroccan mint scent is refreshing, and the lather is moisturizing, not stripping,” McKenzie says. “When the dinner party gets chaotic, this is the easiest way for guests to refresh themselves in the kitchen. The glass bottle is an extra sleek touch.”

There's a reason Drew Barrymore's slow cooker sold out within days of launching. Now, more than a year later, the hype has gone down a bit, making snatching one up a lot easier. It comes in a few delicious colors, but we're partial to the white one, which Barrymore aptly named White Icing.

Keep your favorite spices and garnishes within reach with this beautiful seasoning dish. It's about the capacity of a pinch bowl, but the two walnut wood compartments can hold salt, dried herbs, and even peppercorns and capers separately. Make seasoning food incredibly easy—with one pinch, any meal is elevated.

#6698. Here’s Every Track Lisa Performed on Night 1 of Her Viva La Lisa Residency

Lisa has finally touched down for the first date of her Viva La Lisa residency in Las Vegas. On November 12, the Blackpink rapper made history as the first K-pop act to perform a Las Vegas residency date at The Colosseum at Caesars Palace, and to celebrate the occasion, she hit the stage with several hits from her catalog.

Lisa opened up the show with her recently released single, “Tarantino,” and continued the rest of her 90-minute set with tracks from both of her eras, including her new six-track EP, Press Play. “Cobra,” “Sweat,” “V12,” “Cobra,” and “Cut U Off” were highlighted throughout the setlist, with “SaWaDiKa” closing the evening.

The rapper’s solo album Alter Ego also received its fair share of time throughout the setlist’s five chapters. “Badgrrrl” and “Fxck Up the World” were the first from the album to appear during the show, but deeper into the concert, fan-favorite tracks like “Rockstar,” “New Woman,” “Moonlit Floor,” and “Dream” also appeared in the program.

Longtime fans of Lisa were also treated to hits from the earliest days of her solo career. “Money,” her second independent single released in 2021, had its time to shine during the second chapter of her concert, though fans missed out on hearing her first solo track, “Lalisa,” on the setlist.

Lisa’s Viva La Lisa residency was announced as a four-show engagement but was upgraded to include five more dates due to overwhelming fan demand after the first set of dates sold out in 10 minutes. The remaining dates of the residency will take place on November 13, 14, and 27-29.

#6697. Bulgari Brings High Jewelry to a Palm Beach Stronghold

To celebrate its new boutique, the Italian house unveiled its Eclettica collection at a quintessential South Florida estate.

Above: To celebrate Bulgari’s new collection and boutique, guests roamed the grounds of a waterfront Palm Beach estate, designed by architect Addison Mizner.

Few people shaped the look of South Florida as much as architect Addison Mizner. A pioneer of Spanish Colonial Revival architecture, his Mediterranean style quickly took Palm Beach by storm in the 1920s, as he designed and built more than 50 Florida mansions and villas. And his impact still lingers, as, on Wednesday night, stars like Venus Williams and Chanel Iman spent their evening seated under the tennis pavilion at one of Mizner’s famed waterfront Palm Beach estates.

The crowd was there to celebrate a far newer building in the neighborhood: Bulgari’s Palm Beach boutique, which opened earlier this week. In honor of the store’s debut, the brand brought its newest high jewelry collection, Eclettica, to the United States for the first time.

“Palm Beach embodies a distinctive spirit of effortless sophistication,” said Bulgari CEO Laura Burdese.

Located in a heritage building in Palm Beach’s shopping district, the boutique is modeled after a traditional Italian piazza. Anchored by four travertine columns, the open space encourages clients to move through the maison’s most famous collections. Venini’s classic Serpenti chandelier, made of Murano glass and seen in Bvlgari boutiques worldwide, serves as a focal point in a shape reminiscent of the brand’s many symbolic snakes.

Elsewhere in the store, decorative panels by Milan-based Pictalab depict Floridian ocean and beach views, grounding the Italian maison in its surrounding landscape. Among the art is the Eclettica collection, which borrows its name from eclecticism and stays true to its moniker: mixing inspirations from painting, sculpture, and architecture, translating them into bold, colorful pieces.

It’s a fitting style for Palm Beach, where Mizner’s vibrant Mediterranean and Spanish-influenced designs borrow inspiration from entire eras and regions, giving the area its everlasting mix-master flair.

“We are delighted to bring Eclettica to this extraordinary destination,” Burdese said, “where its bold artistic vision and exceptional craftsmanship find a natural resonance with the city’s vibrant character.”

#6696. Léa Seydoux Isn’t Trying to Act. She’s Trying to Live

The French actress continues her string of emotionally demanding roles in The Unknown and Gentle Monster

Léa Seydoux first stepped into the limelight in Christophe Honoré’s The Beautiful Person, where her role as Junie de Chartres earned her the Trophée Chopard award for rising actors at Cannes. From there, she moved seamlessly between highbrow French cinema and Hollywood, achieving a critical breakthrough with Blue Is the Warmest Color in 2013 and starring alongside Daniel Craig as a Bond girl in Spectre and No Time to Die. Most years, she’s starred in at least two films, and 2026 is no exception: She plays a woman with a man’s soul trapped in her body in Arthur Harari’s psychological fantasy The Unknown, and in Marie Kreutzer’s Gentle Monster, releasing on Netflix on November 18, she’s a pianist who unearths her husband’s hideous crimes. The words thorny and barbed read like understatements for both films, and it’s exactly what interests Seydoux about the stories. Like in life itself, she avoids easy answers, opting instead for exploration.

Seydoux got the chance to explore an especially ambitious idea in The Unknown. It tells the story of David Zimmerman (Niels Schneider), a photographer who wakes up in the body of a woman he sleeps with. Seydoux plays both that woman, Eva, and the man trapped within her body. “I felt the movie was something new to me, to play an impossible idea and make it believable, try to give it a truthful dimension,” Seydoux says. She’s no stranger to high-concept stories, previously starring in Bertrand Bonello’s The Beast and David Cronenberg’s Crimes of the Future. Each time, she imbues these characters with humanity, despite their far-fetched sci-fi dilemmas.

“I like movies that make you think, movies that bring up questions, when there’s a debate around the film,” she says. “What I love the most is to explore human nature, our human condition. This is what cinema allows us to do.”

Seydoux might vehemently love movies, yet acting remains a demanding process for her. She relies on the art form to dig into feelings and emotions. “I don’t feel necessarily very comfortable with acting. It’s not a place where I’m like, ‘I love it,’ but at the same time, I find it a bit painful. It’s more a way to experience things and ideas,” she says.

Gentle Monster provides another knotty premise, this time grounded in reality. It follows Lucy Weiss, a famed experimental pianist who gives up her career to raise her young son with her doting husband, Philip (Laurence Rupp). One day, the police knock on their door, and it’s revealed that Philip is accused of distributing child pornography.

“The subject matter is tough, but the fact that it’s through the woman’s perspective—and not through the victim or perpetrator—it’s through her emotions that the story unfolds [is what interested me],” she says. “Things are not too defined, but it’s also because of the subject matter; it was the only way to talk about it. [Marie] gave me a lot of freedom.”

Born in Paris in 1985, Seydoux spent her childhood wedged among seven siblings and half-siblings. She remembers feeling isolated, practically invisible, as a child. “I was always in the shadows in a way,” she says. “As a kid, I felt transparent. I was never at the center of [my] big family.” This plays a big part in her surprise at her acting career, never thinking of herself as someone deserving of center stage, of fame, of attention. In fact, acting never even crossed her mind until she befriended some actors when she turned 18. “I loved the way they lived their lives,” she says. “I admired their life—the fact that they were free.”

And yet, she rejects the label of “actress.” “I try to be truthful as much as possible,” she says. “I try not to act. This is what I try to be, and I’m more into reaction than action.” She adds, “It’s more reactive than acting.”

For Seydoux, “reacting” and really experiencing life is only possible if she “abandons” herself to her characters. One of the most intense examples of this is Abdellatif Kechiche’s controversial lesbian love story Blue Is the Warmest Color, in which she stars alongside Adèle Exarchopoulos. The film’s schedule stretched from two months to five months, and it has since become notorious for its grueling pace. (The famous sex scene took 10 days alone.) “The greatest and most intense experience I’ve had was with Adèle when we did Blue Is the Warmest Color,” she recalls. “It was crazy, and really the connection between us had to be very, very intense. I think, with Adèle, it’s something that will always be a part of me now. There was an exchange, and it was more than making a film. It was really a life experience.”

One scene from Gentle Monster lingered with me for weeks after I watched it: Lucy’s snotty, tearful confrontation with her husband at their family home as she pleads with him to tell the truth. This close-up of Seydoux not only accentuates her famous blue eyes but also shows her uncanny control of this character’s emotions. It feels, for lack of better words, real. Seydoux partly credits her empathy to being a mother of two children, but this scene goes beyond compassion; it shows how much of herself she gives to the character. Before filming this scene, Kreutzer offered little direction, letting her tap into how the character felt. “I prepare myself, and I have to just let myself go and be in the moment and just open up,” Seydoux says, suggesting that perhaps these emotions otherwise lie dormant within her.

I found this is what Seydoux means by “reaction.” Every role in her varied and extolled filmography functions as an avenue through which she can explore what it means to be human, what it means to feel. Her natural curiosity about the world and its inhabitants existed before she decided to act in her teens and will exist long into the future, even if acting jobs don’t remain on the table. “If it ended tomorrow, I’d be happy,” she says. “I will find a way. Now, I really feel that I’m acting because I still have the opportunity. But I have more things to explore, and I would love to have more experiences.”

#6695. ‘We Just Are So Gagged’: Jordan Firstman and Reggie Absolom on Club Kid’s Success

The film’s stars discuss their excitement, their music tastes, and how they interpret that ending.

This story contains spoilers for Club Kid.

For his debut feature film, Club Kid, Jordan Firstman envisioned a curly-haired boy who could live in a world of adults. The story was well established by the time casting agent Carmen Cuba met British actor Reggie Absolom, who had appeared in Silo and other series. Firstman, who wrote, directed, and starred in the film, knew he was the perfect one to play Arlo, the 10-year-old son that Firstman’s character Peter never knew he had. Firstman and Absolom grew close quickly and developed a unique friendship that hasn’t slowed down post-filming. “We text all day,” Firstman says.

The pair spoke to ELLE about Reggie getting to DJ for the film, whether he’ll have a Radiohead phase, and what the movie says about family.

Jordan Firstman: I had a very extensive list of things I wanted in the character, and my casting director was like, “You really need to manage your expectations because you’re looking for a child actor. Baseline, you have to find someone good.” I also just wanted them to be naturally cool and not feel like a child actor [but] feel like a kid. I wanted them to have curly hair. I wanted them to look enough like me that they could be my kid. In the script, the character is written as half Indian, and she was like, “Babe, you’re just not going to find all of that.”

I found Reggie, and he checked every box. He looked like the kid I had in my head when I wrote it. He also was a really natural actor and nothing felt forced. I sent him a playlist of songs I wrote the movie to, and he told me which ones he liked. I thought he had really good taste, and we just talked for a while and got ice cream. And then it wasn’t until in the process that I found out he was a quarter Indian too. He literally had every single thing that I wanted, and so it did feel like a miracle in a lot of ways.

Reggie Absolom: It was a bit weird at first. But then I became such good friends with everyone anyway and it just felt like I fit in there. It didn’t matter how old I was. I was just friends with everyone, and I was just working like they were as well.

JF: I love sending people that I love music. Reggie’s music taste influenced the characters. He was into Slipknot at the time, so I wrote a little Slipknot into the script, and he also liked Massive Attack and Cocteau Twins, once I sent him my playlist. I wrote that in.

There’s this whole other component of the actual club music, on which we collaborated so closely with David Peck, who’s in the movie [and who wrote songs for the film and performed them as Heather Asylum]. He did Reggie’s whole DJ set and River Moon consulted. We had real DJs in the scene helping me and making sure we had all the right tracks. The music in the movie is everything; it’s the throughline in the story and the construction of the movie.

RA: You get most of the credit. You gave me quite a few songs, like Cocteau Twins ones and really good Massive Attack ones. I was like, okay, I’m going to go deeper into these and listen to all of their albums. Now there’s some songs that I would’ve never really listened to. When I listened to Cocteau Twins, I would’ve been like, “Okay, this is weird.” But now I’m like, “Okay, maybe I like the weird kind of songs.”

JF: I think you’re going to also have a major Radiohead era.

RA: Maybe, but I don’t know. I don’t like Radiohead.

JF: We were going to do “Maybe I’m Amazed” by Paul McCartney, and he wasn’t responding to our emails about licensing it. The day before [we filmed the scene], I was trying to think of other songs, and “Thoroughfare” is my favorite song, I think ever. I feel like I would be that dad that made my kid play Ethel Cain on the piano with me. Then we played it, and it felt really cute and really sincere. We still sang Paul McCartney, but they were like, “You don’t need Paul McCartney. Don’t waste your money.” And “Thoroughfare” is the vibe.

RA: It got me into a lot of different types of music, which I’m happy about. DJing was really fun and something that I’d want to do in the future. Maybe not a full-time job, but something on the side to do, something fun.

RA: It didn’t really influence the roles I wanted to get, but now that I’m in a film that’s done so well, it’s opened up for me to maybe get bigger roles than I would’ve expected before filming this.

RA: The idea of being in a movie, that was the next step for me. I was filming maybe three episodes of a series. I felt like no matter the role, just being in the movie would be the next thing to do. Then the fact that this was a lead role and that I felt similar to Arlo personality-wise in some ways. I felt like I could perform really naturally with the things that Arlo says and the way that he acts.

JF: Kramer vs. Kramer is such a classic and a movie I love. I often reference An Unmarried Woman with Jill Clayburgh. I love her relationship with her daughter in that movie and how they treat each other like adults. Because Peter has no experience with kids, he doesn’t know how to treat them like anything else but how he treats everyone.

JF: Once I started writing it, I started really understanding Peter and Arlo and what they needed. I actually feel like it’s a gift that they didn’t end up together for both of their lives for that time. That phone call [at the end] is so important, because the door opens again once they’re ready. I think it would’ve been too intense for Peter to [raise Arlo full-time]....He still wasn’t completely out of the party scene. He was still selling drugs. He was not ready to really do this in a real way. Arlo really needed a year of knowing true stability and a normal childhood.

But I think what Peter and his community offered Arlo was a very different version of normal. They encouraged creativity. For a traumatized kid, having an art practice is everything. In my darkest times as a kid, I turned to art. To me, it’s a very human ending, and to me it’s hopeful.

RA: Both of them needed to have that break. They’re trying to find themselves and what they should do. Now, another thing, whilst Arlo was with Peter, he was getting no education really. He tried to get him into school.

RA: He just gave up on that. The ending also leaves it up to interpretation what happens to them in the future. Obviously, it could have been like, oh, they rejoin and they’re father and son forever. I think it’s nicer when the film has an ending that’s up to interpretation a bit, where there could be different branches on the ending.

JF: He really enjoyed fatherhood, so I think he will continue his relationship with Arlo and help him through his teen years and grow up.

RA: In my opinion, Peter with a baby, adopting a baby would be a bit, like Edison says, “He’s all prepared. Forget the trauma,” or something like that.

RA: He could do it. He’d need Oscar [Peter’s partner, played by Diego Calva].

JF: No one involved in this project has had this happen to them. Even Cara [Delevingne] has never been a part of something like this. We just are so gagged. We can’t believe this is happening and that people are responding to it in this way. We get standing ovations everywhere we go, and it’s surreal. We don’t take for granted that this is a rare thing that happens.

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.

#6694. Design Experts Reveal When It’s Actually OK to Start Decorating for the Holidays

There’s no hard rule—but your HOA may have one.

Now that the Halloween candy bowls are empty, you might find thoughts of tinsel, garland, and fairy lights dancing in your head, but is it really time to trade your pumpkins for holiday trimmings? Some have strong opinions on the topic, half believing the jolly spirit of the holiday season doesn't start until after Thanksgiving, the other rushing to unbox their silver bells as soon as the clock strikes 12 on November 1—but the choice isn’t always yours to make.

If you belong to a Homeowners’ Association (and over 78.1 million Americans do), the question of when to put up your outdoor décor may be dictated by their guidelines. HOAs tend to have rules about holiday decorations, restrictions covering their location (within the boundaries of your yard, of course), sound level (not too loud), safety (no open flames), light color, and when the decorations can be installed. In some communities, you can even be fined for bringing out the twinkle lights and nativity creche before Thanksgiving—this happened to one Florida man in 2002, when he was hit with a fine of $1,000 for putting his lights up on November 6.

If you don’t live in an HOA, you may not get fined for decorating too early, but you will get judged by neighbors who find pre-Thanksgiving holiday displays a little, well, tacky. To be fair, holding until after Thanksgiving does have its perks—why would you pass on the opportunity to decorate a gorgeous fall tablescape? In the spirit of peace on earth and goodwill toward man, we asked design experts what to do. Here’s what they said.

Additional copy by Meghan Shouse and Alyssa Khan.

You might follow a religious tradition that guides your holiday decorating, like placing the Baby Jesus in the nativity on Christmas Day or keeping the tree up until Epiphany. But when it comes to secular etiquette, there are no hard-and-fast rules about when to start (or stop) decking the halls.

“Most people find it tasteful to stick within the weeks or the month of the holiday itself,” points out Lizzie Post, co-president of The Emily Post Institute and author of Emily Post’s Etiquette—The Centennial Edition. “That said, there is no etiquette that dictates it. It’s the same way you can paint your house neon orange with blue stripes—there’s nothing that says you can’t. It’s really, do you want to be the person who does?”

For designer Tan France, the answer to that question is decidedly yes. For the past decade, he and his husband have been decorating their tree on Halloween and taking it down on Valentine’s Day. “I’m British, and we don’t really do Halloween,” he explains. “When I moved here 16 years ago, I was more obsessed with Christmas. So on Halloween day, when everyone else is crafting their costumes, I want to be decorating the tree. And a tree brings my husband and me a lot of joy when we come down and turn the lights on first thing in the morning. It’s just a lovely thing to perk your day up.”

Do people question France about his decision to put up decorations so early, and for so long? “Oh gosh, yeah, everyone asks that,” he says. “Online, the press, neighbors. And my answer is really simple: Why delay joy?”

If you are jonesing to do some holiday decorating, there's good news: While Christmas might be weeks away, there is one happening right now. It’s called Thanksgiving.

“Anytime before midnight of Thanksgiving is too early to decorate,” insists Wendy Kerrigan, founder of Atelier Design in New Orleans. “Filling the house with Christmas decorations too soon really takes away from the beauty of Thanksgiving, moving your mind into the bustle of Christmas.”

Kerrigan wishes more people would celebrate the fall season for longer; rushing to the tinsel takes away from the beauty of the autumn season, “Thanksgiving can be experienced in a special way too, filling the house with fall colors, fall leaves, gourds, and cornucopia,” says Kerrigan. “Instead of trying to hurry the year along, enjoy each family celebration for what it is.”

Even more, sprinting to the Yuletide season robs the holiday of its own magic. “What makes things special when they’re once a year is that they’re experiences you look forward to,” says Vermont-based interior designer Joshua Smith. “How many holidays can we take at one time? Are we doing Black Friday in July now? Let’s just enjoy the season that we’re in!”

When it comes to holiday decorating, many hands make light work. Several designers say they take advantage of the long holiday weekend and enforced family time to rope in some extra help making spirits bright. “In my parents' home, we start decorating right after Thanksgiving dinner. It’s a rare opportunity for everyone to be together, and my siblings and I stay to help out our parents,” says New York-based designer Mikel Welch. “Plus, it’s a much better tradition than terrorizing sales associates on Black Friday Eve!"

Smith has a plan for bringing the Christmas spirit to his home, without shouldering the burden of doing all the hall-decking himself. Last year, his family decorated their trees over Thanksgiving weekend and made it a tree-decorating contest. With his husband's family in town, “they’ll be divided into three teams, decorating three trees, and I’ll go around at the end judging, and everyone will win prizes. We’re not all going to be together at Christmas, so this activity is a fun way to celebrate the season and not do all the work myself,” he says.

Still, early decorators would argue that the amount of effort needed to set up holiday decor is another reason to put up decorations early—and keep them up late. “This is the most miserable time of the year; it gets dark so early, it’s so darn cold. I just think you should do everything you can to bring yourself joy,” says France. “Why put in all that effort for a few weeks?”

Even those who are normally on the side of waiting to decorate agree that desperate times call for dopamine decorating. Whether it’s the state of your inbox or the state of the world that is stressing you out, if you’re feeling a little blue, go right ahead and pull out the red and green.

“In a perfect calendar, the day after Thanksgiving would be my official start to the holiday season—it's traditionally the day when my family goes out into the woods of Tahoe National Forest to chop down our tree, drag it home, and decorate it,” says designer Noz Nozawa of Noz Design. “But this year, the day after Halloween is when I let the holidays start—there's plenty to worry about, and having my nutcrackers and string lights out a few weeks early can only boost spirits.”

Whenever you decorate, it can be a way to bring fun to a not-always joyful world. “We have no control over a lot of things in our lives, but when we walk through the door of our home, we choose to focus our energy and attention on whether that is on fear or on love and joy,” says Smith. “And this holiday season, I think more than ever we have to focus on ways to bring more light to ourselves and to each other.”

When it comes to early decor, “I can handle it from my neighbor; I cannot handle it from a corporation,” says Post. That may be because we know the Christmas displays popping up in a big-box store alongside the Halloween candy are trying to sell us something, while a neighbor decorating is spreading joy, spending their own time and money to brighten the dark winter nights. For a shop, holiday decorating is mercenary. For an individual, it’s a mitzvah.

If you appreciate the joy inspired by holiday displays and do want to start setting them up early or keep them up late, consider finding a middle ground by leaning toward decor that’s more about lighting up the night, rather than marking a particular occasion. “I see a lot of people in Vermont leaving the actual lights up, not so much like blow-up Santas or things like that, but the actual lights up for the entire winter,” says Post.

If you’re a minimalist (or live in a studio apartment), you don’t need a tree to decorate. “If you don’t have a tree, I’m going to invite you to place a hurricane lamp on your coffee table with a wreath of greenery around it and light a candle in the evening,” says Smith. “Because what we know from neuroscience and neuroaesthetics is, you’ve got to engage the senses to nourish the soul!” Light a candle or turn on some twinkle lights, and you’re liable to spark joy as well.

France would argue that even his trees aren’t occasion-specific. “Here’s the thing: I’m Muslim, and my tree and the lights on our house aren’t necessarily for Christmas,” he explains. “It’s to celebrate Thanksgiving, Christmas, and the New Year. This is the most miserable time of the year. It gets dark so early, it gets light so late, it’s so darn cold. I just think do everything you can to bring yourself joy.”

Though you’d never find them rushing to hang their stockings, the designer duo behind the Kansas City-based firm Kobel + Co, Elizabeth Bennett and Mallory Robins, say there’s one design scheme you can always tap into if you find yourself in the class of outliers running to get decor up before Christmas Eve. “Ralph Lauren Holiday isn't just having a moment; it's a classic holiday go-to for so many reasons,” Bennett explains. “Warm crimsons, rich browns, tactile fabrics, and blue-green plaids make a beautiful holiday palette. Layer in antique silver pieces and simple glass ornaments. A little patina and variation keep everything from feeling too matched. The goal is a warm, welcoming home with pieces that feel collected and personal.” After all, you want it to feel like home, even at Christmas.

#6693. On the Couch: Tina Barney

In honor of her new book, the celebrated photographer shares some select sources of inspiration.

In 1974, Tina Barney moved from New York City to Sun Valley, Idaho, a quiet, picturesque ski hamlet set within the Rocky Mountains. It was there, while taking an art school class on great American photographers, that she began shooting photographs herself. In the five-plus decades that followed, Barney became a legendary American photographer in her own right, with works entering the permanent collections of museums like the Whitney, the Museum of Modern Art, and the Los Angeles County Museum of Art.

The daughter of 1940s fashion model Lillian Fox and granddaughter of the founder of Lehman Brothers, Barney is known for documenting high society with a sharp eye, photographing affluent Americans, European aristocrats, and lavish spaces. She also captured what they wore—and shot for fashion magazines and major designers like Gucci and Bottega Veneta. Barney’s relationship with style, stemming from her mother’s work with fashion photographers like Horst P. Horst and George Hoyningen-Huene, is the subject of her new book Tina Barney: Fashion, out now.

In honor of her book’s release, Barney took our On the Couch survey, sharing a few of her biggest influences and inspirations.

Greatest Influences: I made a film on Jan Groover. I made a film on Horst P. Horst. And you can see the photos he took of my mom in the new book.

Favorite Sofa: My couch has been in this house for decades, and I am probably getting a new one because this one's falling apart. But it is in the right place with the right light. I can watch TV; I can read; I can draw; I can do anything I want from this place. And it's comfortable.

Most beloved buildings: I did a whole book on the Noguchi Museum, a portrait of Isamu Noguchi’s building, in a way, and of his sculpture.

Destination Museum: The Musée d’Orsay. It’s the way the paintings are hung that really interests me. There are artworks placed within little cubicles, and then, of course, there are the most well-known paintings that are just so easy to keep looking at. So the Musée d'Orsay first, followed by almost any other museum in Paris.

Go-to galleries: It depends on the artist that is showing. In New York, I go out to look at galleries every month with a friend. We’ll see what the new shows are, and I always try to learn from what I see.

Favorite Artist: I don't like to name names because there are no favorites. But I just saw a Brice Marden show at Gagosian that was tops.

Favorite getaway: I travel as little as possible right now. I live in Rhode Island part-time, more so than in New York. Here it is quiet.

Best Shop for Gifts: Well, I love agnès b. She’s very supportive of photographers, so that’s how I came to know her, and I still visit her shop in New York.

Style Icons: I don't think of buzzwords like that. But, my mother for sure. Her style was led by her sense of humor, and by her own personality. In those days, you weren't thinking about the fashion designers and who was in and who was out. And so the clothes had much more character to them.

My mother brought me to an Yves Saint Laurent fashion show in Paris when I was about 16, which I remember very well. It was the first show that Yves Saint Laurent ever had on his own. We also saw the designer Guy Laroche; we went in after the show and my mother had a suit made for her, so they measured her. And that was a pretty fantastic experience.

Fashion Designers: I have a picture of me from about when I was age 14 and I'm wearing a suit that looks like a Chanel. And because they were starting to copy her at that point, you could get a viable suit for someone 14 or 16 years old that looked like a Chanel. I love that the style of Chanel has existed for so long, and has not changed that much.

Required Reading: Katie Kitamura. Also, Judith Freeman, my favorite author, who I know is working on a new novel.

Cocktail of Choice: I don’t drink; I never have. I’m like a monk.

Favorite Restaurant: The Olympia Tea Room in Watch Hill, Rhode Island.

Annie Goldsmith is the senior editor and digital lead at ELLE Decor, where she covers design, culture, style, and trends. She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. Her work has also appeared in Vogue, Rolling Stone, and the SF Standard.

#6692. Christy Turlington’s Ruby-Red Lipstick Is the Easy Makeup Secret for Women Over 40

Shop our favorite creamy, hydrating, and smoothing lipstick formulas

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The last few years have been marked by the “Lip Balm Wars.” That is, every brand—from Hailey Bieber’s Rhode to prestige labels like Chanel and Dior—has launched more sheer, glossy, and hydrating formulas than classic lipstick bullets. But even though we all own more than 10 emotional-support lip balms in various shades, flavors, and finishes, there's still nothing more powerful than a statement-making lip. This is especially true for women over 40.

Last night, supermodel Christy Turlington stepped out at the Museum of Modern Art’s 2026 Annual Film Benefit in New York, wearing a knit dress from Matthieu Blazy’s fall 2026 Chanel collection. A dress this graphic and eye-catching needs makeup to balance it, and Turlington paired it with black eyeliner and a glossy ruby-red lipstick.

While we don’t know the exact shade she wore last night, it’s clearly a formula that sits somewhere between a satin lipstick and a gloss-balm, with pigment that doesn’t sacrifice moisture or shine. Turlington is a Lancôme ambassador, so it could be the brand’s Lip Idole Butterglow Hydrating Lip Balm or L’Absolu Rouge Cream Lipstick. Otherwise, Chanel makes a gorgeous option in both the Rouge Coco Flash and Rouge Allure Extrait formulas. These formulas subtly plump and moisturize dry lips while blurring lip lines and imperfections.

Lancôme L'Absolu Rouge Moisturizing Cream Lipstick in 397 Berry Noir

Chanel Hydrating Vibrant Shine Lip Colour

Lancome Lip Idole Squalane-12 Butterglow Hydrating Lip Balm

The best part of this look is that it proves you don’t need to match your lipstick exactly to your outfit, as long as they’re in the same color family. While the Chanel dress is more of an orange-red knit, Turlington’s lipstick is a more neutral ruby that harmonizes with the warm tones and shades in the rest of her look. Plus, raspberry or ruby-red lipstick can brighten your face in seconds, even with little to no other makeup.

Bold lipstick is a bit outside Turlington’s day-to-day comfort zone; she told Bazaar recently that she’s “definitely an old-school Carmex or ChapStick kind of person, because I’m constantly reapplying it to my dry lips, especially in the winter.” The rest of her routine is equally as effortless: “I wash my face in the morning and moisturize a lot. Sometimes I even double-moisturize because, especially at this time of year, I get dry skin. And then, I’ll add a nice little layer of foundation, some blush, and mascara, and that’s kind of it, I’m on my way.”
